哲罗姆有时像奥利金一样，毫无顾忌地歪曲字句与历史，并采用了他那有害的原则：凡字面意义显得可笑或不合体统时，就将其全然摒弃。例如，他将年迈的大卫王的妾、那位书念少女，解释为所罗门所极力颂扬的、永保童贞的上帝智慧（这是效法奥利金，以寓意解释抹去大卫对乌利亚和拔示巴所犯的双重罪行）；230 他又将保罗与彼得之间严肃的争论改成一场假意的交锋，目的在于教导在场的安提阿基督徒；这样便将此事变成一种欺骗性的迁就。奥古斯丁对此种 patrocinium mendacii（为谎言辩护）深感不满，这是理所当然的；他因此与哲罗姆展开书信论争，充分显露了两人的性格。231

值得注意的是，奥古斯丁与哲罗姆在通信讨论的两个释经问题上，互换了立场，各自采取了对方的观点。在关于安提阿事件（《加拉太书》ii. 11–14）的争论中，奥古斯丁代表福音自由与热爱真理的原则，哲罗姆则代表拘守传统教义的原则，以及一种含混的迁就理论；而在关于《七十士译本》权威的争论中，哲罗姆坚持真正的进步，奥古斯丁却坚持倒退与错误的传统主义。后来，两人各自都看见了自己的错误，并至少在一定程度上将其放弃。

解释先知书时，哲罗姆看出了太多针对他那个时代异端的暗示（正如路德处处看出针对教皇派、狂热派和宗派分子的暗示一样）；另一方面，他从奥利金那里承袭了反对一切千禧年论 (chiliasm) 的热忱，因此对于主再来时万物终结的指涉，又看得太少。例如，他甚至将《马太福音》第二十四章中基督论末世的讲论，以及《帖撒罗尼迦后书》中保罗关于大罪人的预言，都局限于耶路撒冷的毁灭。

属于广义释经著作的，还有《希伯来名字释义》(On the Interpretation of the Hebrew Names)；这是一部解释新旧约专名词源的词典，在当时颇为有用，但在许多方面存在缺陷，如今已无价值；232 此外，他还意译了优西比乌的《地名录》(Onomasticon)，这是一种按字母顺序编排的圣经地志，至今对于古物研究仍有价值。233

  1. 历史著作，其中有些我们已在别处论及，对于教父与圣徒的历史、基督教文学史以及道德史，都具有重要意义。

其中首先应提到的，是他用拉丁文对优西比乌希腊文《编年史》(Chronicle) 所作的自由改写与续编；也就是将世界史与教会史上最重要的事件，按年代编成表格，直至379年。234 哲罗姆在君士坦丁堡与拿先斯的贵格利 (Gregory Nazianzen) 同住期间（公元380年），十分仓促地口授了这部著作。尽管其中错误很多，它仍是对拉丁文学一项非常有用而值得称许的贡献，并成为整个中世纪西方基督教世界有限历史知识的主要来源之一。阿基坦的普罗斯珀 (Prosper Aquitanus) 是奥古斯丁的朋友，也是在高卢反对半伯拉纠派、捍卫白白恩典教义的人；他将《编年史》续写至449年；后来的作者又将其续至六世纪中叶。

更具原创性的是《名家录》(Catalogue of Illustrious Authors)，235 哲罗姆应任官的朋友德克斯特 (Dexter) 之请，于狄奥多西 (Theodosius) 在位第十年（公元392及393年）编成。236 这部著作开创了神学文学史的先河，以一百三十五章，为从使徒到哲罗姆本人的一百三十五位教会作家撰写简传，并介绍他们最重要的著作。其用意之一，是驳斥塞尔苏斯 (Celsus)、波菲利 (Porphyry)、尤利安 (Julian) 及其他异教徒对基督徒无知的指控。哲罗姆当时尚未成为如此激烈的异端憎恶者，对于奥利金和优西比乌等人的评价，还相当公允而宽厚。237 然而，他的许多小传过于简短贫乏；甚至对居普良 (Cyprian)、亚他那修 (Athanasius)、大巴西尔 (Basil the Great)、尼撒的贵格利 (Gregory of Nyssa)、伊皮法纽 (Epiphanius)、安布罗斯 (Ambrose) 和金口约翰（卒于407年）这样重要的人物，也不例外。238 至于与他同时代、年纪较轻的奥古斯丁，虽然当时已写了几部哲学、释经及论战著作，他却完全略而不提。

此后，马赛的半伯拉纠派人物根纳迪乌斯 (Gennadius)、塞维利亚的伊西多尔 (Isidore)、伊尔德丰苏斯 (Ildefonsus) 及其他人，以同样的精神续编《名家录》，一直延续到中世纪。

哲罗姆还为著名隐士底比斯的保罗 (Paul of Thebes，公元375年)、希拉里翁 (Hilarion) 和曾被囚禁的马尔库斯 (Malchus，公元390年) 撰写传记，文笔十分优美生动，却夹杂许多荒诞和迷信的成分，并对修道生活推崇备至；他写这些传记，正是为了提倡这种生活。239 当时人们争相阅读这些著作，热切程度不亚于阅读小说。这些传记，以及为悼念已故友人尼波提安 (Nepotian)、卢基尼乌斯 (Lucinius)、利娅 (Lea)、布拉西拉 (Blasilla)、保利娜 (Paulina)、保拉 (Paula) 和马尔切拉 (Marcella) 等人而写的几封悼亡书信，都是修辞华丽、宣扬禁欲的圣徒传记杰作。它们开启了中世纪的圣徒传说文学，将历史与虚构不加区别地混在一起，并为民众的造就而牺牲历史真实。

  1. 在教义与伦理方面的论战著作中，240 有些涉及阿里乌派争论，有些涉及奥利金主义争论，有些则涉及伯拉纠派争论。第一类包括《驳分裂派路西弗派对话录》(Dialogue against the schismatic Luciferians)；241 这是哲罗姆在叙利亚旷野生活期间（公元379年），因安提阿的米利提乌 (Meletius) 分裂而写的；此外还有他翻译狄迪穆 (Didymus) 的《论圣灵》(On the Holy Ghost)，此书在罗马开始翻译，在伯利恒完成。他的《驳耶路撒冷主教约翰》(Against Bishop John of Jerusalem，公元399年)，以及致昔日友人鲁菲努斯 (Rufinus) 的三卷《辩护书》(Apology，公元402—403年)，都是针对奥利金主义而作。242 第三类则包括三卷《驳伯拉纠派对话录》(Dialogue against the Pelagians，公元415年)。其他论战著作，如《驳赫尔维迪乌斯》(Against Helvidius，写于383年)、《驳约维尼安》(Against Jovinian，公元393年) 及《驳维吉兰提乌斯》(Against Vigilantius，于406年一夜之间匆匆口授而成)，在性质上部分属于教义，部分属于伦理，主要致力于维护马利亚无玷的童贞、独身、守夜、圣物崇拜及修道生活。

这些论战著作的内容，我们已在适当之处述及；总的说来，它们并未给作者增添多少光彩，反而与他作为罗马教会主要圣徒之一的声誉形成鲜明对照。这些著作显示出他精通辩护者的一切技巧和辩证论者的一切搏斗手段，同时又充满无穷的暴烈与狂热；为摧毁对手，他毫不犹豫地动用机锋、嘲笑、讽刺、猜疑和诽谤等一切武器，甚至在对手死后仍穷追不舍。243 这些著作的内容也不足以弥补这些缺陷。因为哲罗姆并不是一位富有原创性、深刻、系统或前后一贯的思想家，因此很不适合做教义神学家。在阿里乌派争论中，他不愿参与任何关于 οὐσία* 与 ὑπόστασις* 之区别的讨论，而将这个重要问题交给罗马主教达马苏 (Damasus) 裁决；在奥利金主义争论中，他猛烈谴责一切奥利金派，因而不得不与自己先前的看法相矛盾，因为他曾尊崇奥利金为使徒之后最伟大的教师；在伯拉纠派争论中，他主要受到个人因素的影响，被拉向奥古斯丁一边，却只走到半途；因为，尽管他始终确信罪的普遍性，244 但在意志自由和预定的问题上，他采取了神人合作说或半伯拉纠派的观点，此后直到伊拉斯谟 (Erasmus) 的时代，仍一直深受半伯拉纠派的推重。245

作为道德学家和实践神学家，他也同样不能令人满意。他没有连贯的道德教义体系，也未深入基督徒生活的根基与核心，而是在禁欲主义和决疑论的外围游移。他随从时代精神，将宗教的本质看作以修道方式逃离世界，蔑视上帝所设立的自然秩序，尤其是婚姻；他甚至完全颠倒健全的原则，提倡将禁欲者的不洁当作内在纯洁的外在标志。246 他对婚姻的看法十分低下，认为婚姻不过是为了增加童贞者而不得不容忍的一种恶。他从保罗在《哥林多前书》vii. 1所说的“男不近女倒好”，推出一个全无根据的结论：“所以，亲近女人就是坏的；因为好的反面只有坏。”他又将主对怀孕和奶孩子者所宣告的灾祸（《马太福音》xxiv. 19），解释为对一切怀孕、婴孩啼哭，以及婚姻生活的一切烦扰与果实的谴责。彼得曾经结婚这一令他不快的事实，他则试图用一种毫无根据的假定来冲淡：使徒撇下渔网时，也撇下了妻子；此外，“他必定已经以殉道之血，洗去了婚姻生活的污点。”247

在一封致青年尼波提安 (Nepotian) 的信中，248 他提出如下忠告；这封信在其他方面却十分优美，内容丰富：“你的住处应当很少、甚至从不让女人来访。对于基督的一切女儿和童贞女，你要么一概不加理会，要么一视同仁地爱她们。不仅如此，不要与她们住在同一屋檐下，也不要信赖她们以往的贞洁；你不可能比大卫更圣洁，也不可能比所罗门更有智慧。永远不要忘记，是一个女人使乐园的居民被逐出自己的居所。患病时，任何弟兄，或你的姊妹，或你的母亲，都可以照料你；若没有这样的亲属，教会本身也供养着许多年老妇人，你可以用她们所乐于接受的施舍，同时酬答她们的护理。我知道，有些人身体固然健康，心灵却患了病。无论如何，由一个你常常见到其面容的人来服侍你，都是危险的。如果你因教士的职务必须探访寡妇或少女，切不可独自进入她家。只带那些与你作伴不会使你蒙羞的人同行；只带某个诵经员、辅祭或唱诗员，他的装饰不在衣服，而在善良的品行；他不用卷发针卷曲头发，却在整个举止中显出贞洁。但私下里，或在没有见证人的时候，绝不要让自己与女人相处。”

不过，这类劝诫完全符合那个时代的精神，也部分出于哲罗姆本人青年时期的痛苦经验，以及日趋衰落的罗马帝国中社会生活彻底败坏的状况。

哲罗姆在提倡这些禁欲主义的极端做法时，也不忘挥动尖刻讽刺的鞭子，责打教士和修士的过失。他的著作中还处处散布着珍珠般优美的道德格言，以及劝人轻看世界、敬虔度日的雄辩训言。249

  1. 哲罗姆的书信，尽管有种种缺点，仍然极富教益与趣味，在行文的流畅和措辞的优雅上，不逊于西塞罗 (Cicero) 的书信。瓦拉尔西 (Vallarsi) 首次在其所编版本的第一卷中，按年代排列这些书信，使从前的编号（甚至本笃会版的编号）都不再适用。他共计收录一百五十封，其中包括同时代人的若干书信，如伊皮法纽 (Epiphanius)、亚历山大的提阿非罗 (Theophilus of Alexandria)、奥古斯丁、达马苏 (Damasus)、帕马基乌斯 (Pammachius) 和鲁菲努斯 (Rufinus) 的信；有些直接写给哲罗姆，有些则讨论他所关心的事情。哲罗姆自己的书信写给罗马主教达马苏、元老帕马基乌斯、诺拉主教保利努斯 (Paulinus of Nola)、亚历山大的提阿非罗、埃万格鲁斯 (Evangelus)、鲁菲努斯、赫利奥多鲁斯 (Heliodorus)、里帕里乌斯 (Riparius)、尼波提安 (Nepotianus)、奥凯阿努斯 (Oceanus)、阿维图斯 (Avitus)、鲁斯提库斯 (Rusticus)、高登提乌斯 (Gaudentius) 和奥古斯丁等朋友，也有些写给保拉 (Paula)、尤斯托基乌姆 (Eustochium)、马尔切拉 (Marcella)、富里娅 (Furia)、法比奥拉 (Fabiola) 和德米特里娅斯 (Demetrias) 等著名的禁欲妇女与童贞女。这些书信几乎涉及当时震动基督教世界的一切哲学与宗教实践问题，忠实地反映了哲罗姆及其时代的美德、缺点和显著的矛盾。

在神论与基督论上持正统立场，在人论上持半伯拉纠派立场，在教会与传统的教义上倾向罗马，在末世论上反对千禧年说，在伦理上主张律法主义与禁欲主义；猛烈攻击一切异端，狂热地为修道主义的一切极端做法辩护——这样的哲罗姆，在整个天主教中世纪被尊为基督教学术与教会学术的主保圣徒，并被尊为仅次于奥古斯丁的“教会最伟大的博士”（maximus doctor ecclesiae）。然而，由于他热爱圣经、诉诸原文、作出经典的圣经翻译，并在释经上有多方面的贡献，他也为宗教改革提供了重大的助力。在拉丁教父中，作为学者和作家，他至今仍居首位；作为有影响力的神学家，则居第二位（仅次于奥古斯丁）。但就道德品格而言，他显然远逊于希拉里 (Hilary)、安布罗斯 (Ambrose) 和利奥一世 (Leo I.) 等许多人；即使按照罗马禁欲主义的标准，也只能在极有限的意义上将他视为圣徒。250

§ 178. 奥古斯丁 (Augustine)

I. 《希坡主教圣奥勒留·奥古斯丁著作集》(S. Aurelii Augustini Hipponensis episcopi Opera … Post Lovaniensium theologorum recensionem［鲁汶神学家校订本于1577年在安特卫普出版，共11卷］castigatus［指第一卷，即 tomus primus，等等］denuo ad MSS. codd. Gallicanos, etc. Opera et studio monachorum ordinis S. Benedicti e congregatione S. Mauri［圣莫尔会本笃会修士依据法国等地抄本重新校订；编者为 Fr. 德尔福 (Fr. Delfau)、Th. 布朗潘 (Th. Blampin)、P. 库斯坦 (P. Coustant) 和 Cl. 盖尼耶 (Cl. Guesnié)］)。巴黎，1679—1700年，xi卷，合订为8册对开本。同一版本加以增补，于1700—1703年在安特卫普重印，分12部分，合订为9册对开本；又于1729—1734年在威尼斯重印，xi卷，合订为8册对开本（我通常据此版引文；不可将它与1756—1769年另一种威尼斯版混淆，后者共xviii卷，四开本，印刷错误甚多）；1807年又在巴萨诺 (Bassano) 出版，共18卷；高姆兄弟 (Gaume fratres) 于1836—1839年在巴黎出版，xi卷，分22部分（版本极为精美）；最后由 J. P. 米涅 (J. P. Migne) 于1841—1849年在小蒙鲁日 (Petit-Montrouge) 出版，xii卷（《拉丁教父集》〔Patrol. Lat.〕第xxxii—xlvii卷）。米涅版（我也偶尔使用）在补编卷（第xii卷）中，收有舍讷曼 (Schönemann)《拉丁教父历史文献书目》(Bibliotheca historico-literaria Patrum Lat.，第ii卷，莱比锡〔Lips.〕，1794年) 中极有价值的《奥古斯丁生平、著作与版本文献考》(Notitia literaria de vita, scriptis et editionibus Aug.)、诺里修斯 (Norisius) 的《奥古斯丁辩护》(Vindiciae Augustinianae)，以及由丰塔尼尼 (Fontanini) 和安杰洛·迈 (Angelo Mai) 首次刊布的奥古斯丁著作。然而，一部完全可靠的奥古斯丁著作校勘本，至今仍付阙如。有关本笃会版价值的争论，见米涅版补编第xii卷，第40页及后；另见蒂利耶 (Thuillier)：《本笃会神父所编圣奥古斯丁新版史》(Histoire de la nouvelle ed. de S. Aug. par les PP. Bénédictins)，巴黎，1736年。奥古斯丁著作的第一种印本于1489—1495年在巴塞尔出版；另一版于1509年出版，共11卷（我藏有此版一部，用哥特字体印刷，但缺扉页）；随后是伊拉斯谟 (Erasmus) 编订、弗罗本尼乌斯 (Frobenius) 出版的版本，巴塞尔，1528—1529年，共10卷，对开本；又有《鲁汶版》(Editio Lovaniensis)，即鲁汶神学家所编版本，安特卫普，1577年，共11卷，此后屡次重印。奥古斯丁的若干著作也多次单独刊行，尤其是《忏悔录》(Confessions) 和《上帝之城》(City of God)。1794年以前各版的完整目录，参见舍讷曼《书目》(Bibliotheca) 第ii卷，第73页及后。

  1. 波西狄乌斯 (Possidius，卡拉马主教〔Calamensis episcopus〕，奥古斯丁的学生和朋友)：《奥古斯丁传》(Vita Augustini，篇幅简短，却真实可靠；写于432年，即奥古斯丁死后两年；载本笃会版第x卷附录，第257—280页，几乎所有其他版本也都收入)。本笃会编者 (Benedictini Editores)：《主要依据奥古斯丁本人著作编成的奥古斯丁传》(Vita Augustini ex ejus potissimum scriptis concinnata)，共8卷（考订极详，篇幅宏大），载本笃会版第xi卷，第1—492页（米涅重印本第i卷，第66—578页）。其他传记作者有：蒂耶蒙 (Tillemont，《回忆录》〔Mém.〕第xiii卷)；埃利·迪潘 (Ellies Dupin，《教会作家新文库》〔Nouvelle bibliothèque des auteurs ecclésiastiques〕第ii、iii卷)；P. 贝尔 (P. Bayle，《历史与批评辞典》〔Dictionnaire historique et critique〕，“奥古斯丁”条)；雷米·塞利耶 (Remi Ceillier，《圣书与教会作家通史》〔Histoire générale des auteurs sacrés et ecclés.〕第xi、xii卷)；凯夫 (Cave，《教父传》〔Lives of the Fathers〕第ii卷)；克洛特 (Kloth，《圣奥古斯丁》〔Der heil. Aug.〕，亚琛，1840年，2卷)；伯林格 (Böhringer，《传记体教会史》〔Kirchengeschichte in Biographien〕第i卷，第iii部分，第99页及后)；普茹拉 (Poujoulat，《圣奥古斯丁传》〔Histoire de S. Aug.〕，巴黎，1843年及1852年，2卷；Fr. 赫特尔 (Fr. Hurter) 所译德文本，沙夫豪森〔Schaffh.〕，1847年，2卷)；艾森巴特 (Eisenbarth，斯图加特〔Stuttg.〕，1853年)；菲利普·沙弗 (Ph. Schaff，《圣奥古斯丁》〔St. Augustine〕，柏林，1854年；英文本，纽约及伦敦，1854年)；C. 宾德曼 (C. Bindemann，《圣奥古斯丁》〔Der heil. Aug.〕第i卷，柏林，1844年；第ii卷，1855年，未完)。布劳内 (Braune)：《莫尼卡与奥古斯丁》(Monica und Augustin)，格里马，1846年。另参§ 146，第783页所列文献。

论述奥古斯丁哲学的，有布鲁克尔 (Brucker)、滕内曼 (Tennemann)、里克斯纳 (Rixner)、H. 里特尔 (H. Ritter，第vi卷，第153—443页)、胡贝尔 (Huber，《教父哲学》〔Philosophie der Kirchenväter〕) 等人的大型哲学史著作，以及下列著作：特奥德·冈高夫 (Theod. Gangauf)：《圣奥古斯丁的形而上学心理学》(Metaphysische Psychologie des heil. Augustinus)，第1部分，奥格斯堡，1852年。T. 泰里 (T. Théry)：《圣奥古斯丁的哲学与文学天才》(Le génie philosophique et littéraire de saint Augustin)，巴黎，1861年。弗洛特神父 (Abbé Flottes)：《圣奥古斯丁研究：他的天才、心灵与哲学》(Études sur saint Aug., son génie, son âme, sa philosophie)，巴黎，1861年。努里松 (Nourrisson)：《圣奥古斯丁的哲学》(La philosophie de saint Augustin，法兰西学会获奖著作)，第2版，巴黎，1866年，2卷。

撰写自己的生平，是一项冒险而微妙的事业，即使这生平堪称自然或上帝恩典的杰作，因而极值得记述，也是如此。在所有自传中，没有一部像圣奥古斯丁的自传那样，巧妙地避开了虚荣与自夸的暗礁；也没有一部凭借其诚实与谦卑，赢得如此多的敬重与爱戴。

他在四十六岁那年写成《忏悔录》(Confessions)，当时起初爱心的热情仍在燃烧，书中充满圣灵的火焰与膏泽。这是一篇崇高的倾诉，奥古斯丁如同《诗篇》第五十一篇中的大卫，在本代和后世的人面前，毫无保留地向上帝承认自己青年时期的罪；同时，这也是一首颂赞上帝恩典的诗歌，这恩典领他出黑暗入光明，并呼召他在基督的国中事奉。251 在这里，我们看见这位历代伟大的教会教师“俯伏尘埃，与上帝交谈，沐浴在他的爱中；他的读者不过像影子一般浮现在他面前”。他将一切尊荣、一切伟大、一切美好都从自己身上卸下，感恩地放在至慈悲者的脚前。读者处处感到，基督教不是梦幻，也不是错觉，而是真理与生命，因而情不自禁地一同敬拜上帝奇妙的恩典。

奥勒留·奥古斯丁 (Aurelius Augustinus) 于354年11月13日出生，252 出生地塔加斯特 (Tagaste) 是北非富饶的努米底亚 (Numidia) 行省一个不起眼的村庄，距希坡雷吉乌斯 (Hippo Regius) 不远。他从异教徒父亲帕特里修斯 (Patricius)253 那里承袭了热烈的感性，从基督徒母亲莫尼卡 (Monica) 那里承袭了对上帝深切的渴慕。莫尼卡是基督教史上最高贵的妇女之一，才智与灵性俱高，虔诚炽热，情感极其温柔，爱能胜过一切。奥古斯丁在这句话中，庄严地表达了这种渴慕：“你为你自己造了我们；我们的心若不安息在你里面，就不得安宁。”254 这种渴慕，以及他对耶稣那甘甜而圣洁之名的敬畏，虽然一度被挤到心灵的深处，却始终伴随着他：他在马道拉 (Madaura) 和迦太基 (Carthage) 的学校求学，前往罗马与米兰，以及在肉欲享乐、摩尼教 (Manichaean) 的伪智慧、学院派 (Academic) 的怀疑论和柏拉图 (Platonic) 的唯心主义迷宫中长期徘徊时，都是如此。直到最后，他母亲的祈祷、安布罗斯的讲道、圣安东尼 (St. Anthony) 的传记，尤其是保罗的书信，仿佛圣灵手中的诸般器皿，在这个三十三岁的人身上成就了奇妙的改变，使他成为整个基督教世界不可估量的祝福，甚至使他青年时期的罪恶与谬误也为真理效力。255

如此多的祈祷与眼泪所生的儿子，不可能灭亡；这位忠心的母亲在灵里为他受生产之苦，比当初肉身生他时所受的痛苦更深。256 为了鼓励后世的母亲，上帝使她得以在临终前不久，看见自己的祈祷与盼望蒙了应允；她不必重返尘世的家乡，便能欢然离世。因为莫尼卡在归乡途中，于台伯河口的奥斯提亚 (Ostia) 去世，享年五十六岁，临终时躺在儿子的怀中；此前她曾与儿子作了一次荣耀的交谈，超越时空的界限，预尝圣徒永恒的安息。她并不因死在异乡而遗憾，因为她并未远离上帝，上帝必在末日使她复活。她最后的请求是：“把我的身体葬在任何地方都可以，不必为此烦恼；我只求这一件事：无论你们在哪里，都要在我上帝的祭坛前记念我。”257 奥古斯丁在《忏悔录》中，为莫尼卡树立了一座永不朽坏、最为崇高的纪念碑。

若说除了保罗在大马士革路上的归信之外，还有哪一次归信如此彻底而富有成果，那就是奥古斯丁的归信。386年9月，他在米兰附近卡西基亚库姆别墅 (Villa Cassiciacum) 的花园里，正经历心智与心灵最剧烈的挣扎——新生命诞生的阵痛——这时听见一个孩子传来神圣的声音：“拿起来，读吧！”于是他便“披戴主耶稣基督”（《罗马书》十三14）。他曾发出这样感人的哀叹：“我爱你太迟了，你这亘古常新之美；我爱你太迟了！看哪，你原在我里面，我却在外面，在那里寻找你。我以自己的丑陋，一头投入你美好的受造之物中；因为你与我同在，我却不与你同在！那些东西使我远离你，而它们若不在你里面，便根本不能存在！你呼唤，你大声呼喊，冲破了我的耳聋。你闪耀，你发光，驱散了我的眼瞎。你嘘气，我便吸气，将你吸入。我尝了你的滋味，便饥渴不已。你触摸我，我便燃起对你平安的渴望。倘若有一天，我能以整个内在生命活在你里面，痛苦与烦恼便要离我而去；我完全被你充满，一切对我都将成为生命。”

387年复活节主日，他与一同归信的朋友阿利庇乌斯 (Alypius)，以及自己的非婚生子阿德奥达图斯 (Adeodatus，意为上帝所赐)，在米兰一同领受安布罗斯的洗礼。这为他内在的转变印上了神圣的印记。他与世界彻底决裂；放弃了在罗马和米兰所从事的、前程光明而收入丰厚的修辞教师职业；变卖财物，周济穷人；从此将自己罕有的才干专用于事奉基督，并忠于这事奉，直到最后一息。他以最温柔的感情敬爱母亲；母亲去世后，他第二次前往罗马，逗留数月，撰写著作，捍卫真正的基督教，反对虚假的哲学和摩尼教异端。返回非洲后，他与朋友阿利庇乌斯和埃沃狄乌斯 (Evodius) 在故乡塔加斯特的一处庄园里，度过了三年潜心默想与著述的隐居生活。

随后，在391年，他在努米底亚的滨海城市希坡雷吉乌斯 (Hippo Regius，今博纳〔Bona〕)，违背自己的意愿，因民众的呼声而被选为长老；正如居普良和安布罗斯的类似经历一样，这呼声后来证明就是上帝的声音。395年，他又被选为该城的主教。他在此劳苦三十八年，直到去世，使这里成为西方基督教世界的思想中心。258

他的外在生活极其简朴，带有温和的禁欲色彩。他与教士们同住一所房屋，仿照使徒实行财物公用，并使这所房屋成为一所神学院，从中培养出十位主教和许多低级教士。妇女不得进入他的住所，连他的姊妹也不例外，只有旁人在场时才能见他。但他创立了女子修道团体，其中一所由他的姊妹主持；她是一位圣洁的寡妇。259 他曾在一次讲道中说，他在任何地方都未见过比修道院里更好的人，也未见过比那里更坏的人。他将教士生活与修道生活结合起来，无意中成为奥古斯丁修会 (Augustinian order) 的创始人；这个修会后来为世界带来了改革者路德。他穿着东方共住修士的黑色服装，戴兜帽，束皮带。他几乎完全以蔬菜为食，用读书或自由交谈为共同进餐增添情趣；交谈有一条规矩，绝不可议论不在场者的品格。他在餐桌上刻下这副对句：

“Quisquis amat dictis absentum rodere vitam,

Hanc mensam vetitam noverit esse sibi.”

“凡爱以言语诋毁不在场者的为人，

当知此桌不许他入席。”

他常常连续五天讲道，有时一天讲两次，并以此作为讲道的目标：使众人与他、他与众人一同活在基督里面。他在非洲无论走到哪里，人们都恳请他宣讲救恩之道。260 虽然他最大的喜乐在于默想，他仍忠实地处理职分所涉及的外务。他尤其关怀穷人，并像安布罗斯一样，在急需之时命人熔化教会的器皿，用以赎回俘虏。但凡遗赠有损法定继承人的权益，他便拒绝接受。他还称赞迦太基的主教奥勒留 (Aurelius)：有一人将财产遗赠教会，后来妻子意外为他生了儿女，奥勒留便不待请求，主动归还了那份财产。

奥古斯丁的劳作远远超出了他那小小的教区。他是当时北非乃至整个西方教会的思想领袖。他积极关注一切神学和教会问题。他是捍卫正统教义、抵抗摩尼派 (Manichaean)、多纳徒派 (Donatist) 和伯拉纠派 (Pelagian) 的健将。当时大公教会反对异端与分裂的全部论战力量，都集中在他身上；也在他身上赢得了对它们的胜利。

晚年，他批判性地回顾了自己的著述，并在《订正录》(Retractations) 中作了彻底的审查。他以温和精神撰写的、反对半伯拉纠派 (Semi-Pelagians) 的最后几部论战著作，也出于这一时期。他独自承担主教职务，直到七十二岁，那时会众一致推选他的朋友赫拉克利乌斯 (Heraclius) 为他的助手和继任者。

他的晚年饱受日益加重的身体衰弱之苦；汪达尔 (Vandals) 蛮族所带来的无可言喻的惨状，也使他忧心。他们一路得胜，入侵他的国土，毫不留情地摧毁城邑、村庄和教堂，甚至围攻设防坚固的希坡城。261 然而，他仍忠心坚持自己的工作。他在生命的最后十天闭门独处，在祈祷、流泪和反复诵读忏悔诗篇中度日；他曾命人将这些诗篇写在床上方的墙上，好使它们常在眼前。他就这样以悔罪的行动结束了自己的一生。在围城的恐怖和人民的绝望之中，他无从想到，自己已为未来撒下何等丰盛的种子。

在希坡被围的第三个月，即430年8月28日，他年届七十六岁，心智仍然清明，在许多朋友和门生的陪伴下，安详而喜乐地进入了他长久渴慕的永恒。他在《默想录》(Meditations) 中写道：262 “全能的上帝啊，你殿中的居所何等奇妙，何等美丽可爱！我满怀炽热的渴望，要在你的洞房中瞻仰你的荣美……耶路撒冷啊，上帝的圣城，基督亲爱的新妇，我心爱你，我的灵魂早已长久叹慕你的美丽！……万王之王亲自在你中间，他的儿女住在你的城墙之内。那里有天使歌唱的诗班，有天上国民的团契。凡走完这悲苦尘世旅程、进入你喜乐的人，都在那里同赴婚筵。那里有目光远大的先知之列，有十二使徒之数，有无数殉道者和圣洁认信者得胜的军旅。完全而圆满的爱在那里掌权，因为上帝是一切，又在一切之中。他们爱他、赞美他，永远赞美他、爱他……当我这可怜的身体解体时，……若我也能站在我的王、我的上帝面前，得见他的荣耀，我也必蒙福，完全而永远蒙福，正如他亲自俯就应许说：‘父啊，我在哪里，愿你所赐给我的人也同我在那里，叫他们看见我创世以前同你所有的荣耀。’”这种对天上耶路撒冷的渴慕，在赞美诗《乐园的荣耀与喜乐》(De gloria et gaudiis Paradisi) 中得到了宏伟的表达：

“Ad perennis vitae fontem mens sativit arida,”

“干渴的心灵渴慕永生的泉源，”

这首诗被收入奥古斯丁的《默想录》，其构想也部分源于他，不过直到很久以后，才由彼得·达米安尼 (Peter Damiani) 写成诗歌的形式。263

他没有留下遗嘱，因为他自愿贫穷，除了藏书之外，没有任何属世财产可以处置；他将藏书留给教会，而这些书也幸而躲过了亚流派 (Arian) 蛮族的劫掠，保存下来。264

他去世后不久，希坡便被汪达尔人攻陷并摧毁。265 罗马人失去了非洲。几十年后，整个西罗马帝国也倾覆成了废墟。非洲教会的鼎盛之时，正是它开始衰落之日。但奥古斯丁的工作却不能消亡。他的思想如同活的种子，落在欧洲的土壤里，在他从未听闻的民族与国土中，结出了丰硕的果实。266

奥古斯丁——通常画像中那位举目仰望、左手执笔、右手捧着一颗燃烧之心的人——是一位第一流的哲学与神学天才，像金字塔一般高耸于他的时代之上，威然俯瞰后世各个世纪。他的心智异常丰饶而深邃，大胆而高远；更可贵的是，他还有一颗充满基督徒之爱与谦卑的心。他理所当然地与古今最伟大的哲学家并列。无论在宽阔的大道还是狭窄的小径上，无论在令人眩晕的阿尔卑斯山巅，还是在令人敬畏的思辨深渊中，凡前后哲学思想家踏足之处，我们都能遇见他。作为神学家，他是facile princeps（无可争议的第一人），至少没有一位教父、经院学者或改革者超越他。他以君王般的慷慨，随手散布思想，使别的国土和后来的时代发生了强大的运动。他将德尔图良的创造力与居普良的教会精神、希腊教会的思辨才智与拉丁教会的实践智慧结合起来。他是一位十足的基督教哲学家，也是一位富于哲学思想的神学家。反复与最艰难的思想问题搏斗，并尽可能充分地理解上帝所启示的信仰内容，既是他的需要，也是他的喜乐。267 诚然，他始终主张信仰的优先地位，正如他的格言所说：Fides praecedit intellectum（信仰先于理解）；他与先前的神学家一样，援引《以赛亚书》七9在七十士译本（LXX.）中的那句名言：“Nisi credideritis, non intelligetis.”（你们若不信，就不能理解。）但对他而言，信仰本身就是理性的一种活动，因此从信仰到知识，必然有一个过渡。268 他总是透过表面，探究行为背后隐秘的动机，以及纷繁事件中的普遍规律。形而上学家与基督徒信仰者在他身上融为一体。他的meditatio（默想）极其自然地转为oratio（祈祷），他的oratio（祈祷）又转为meditatio（默想）。他的思想既深刻，又同样清晰敏锐。他是一位极其娴熟而卓有成效的辩证论者，在提出论据、回应对手的反驳方面，都有取之不尽的资源。

他为拉丁文学增添了丰富的格言，其优美、独创而意蕴深厚的警句之多，超过任何古典作家或其他教会教师。269

他几乎对教会的每一项教义都作出了创造性而具有决定意义的贡献，有些由他完成，有些由他推进。他体系的中心，是上帝在基督里白白赐下的救赎恩典，这恩典藉着现实的、历史中的教会运行。在罪与恩典的教义上，他是福音派的，或说是保罗式的；但在教会论上，他则是大公教会的（即古代大公教会，而非罗马天主教）。保罗式的要素主要在伯拉纠派论争中显明，大公教会的要素则主要在多纳徒派论争中显明；但两者又彼此制约。

鲍尔博士 (Dr. Baur) 错将自由看作奥古斯丁体系的根本观念（这个观念其实更适用于伯拉纠的体系），并据此对奥古斯丁与奥利金 (Origen) 作了一番巧妙、却仅有一半正确的比较。他说：270“在古代教会教师中，就才智以及见解的宏伟与一贯而言，没有谁比奥古斯丁更配与奥利金并列；尽管二人在个性和思想方式上迥然有别，却没有谁与奥利金如此相似。他们二人远远超越其时代，最清楚的明证就在于：在头六个世纪的所有神学家中，唯独他们创立了各自独特的体系，各从一个确定的观念出发，并贯彻到底；这一事实也表明，两种体系之间有多少相似之处。两个体系同样以自由的观念为基础；在二者之中，都有一个决定人类生活全部发展的特定行动；而且，这一行动都远在个人的时间性意识之外；唯一的差别是，在一个体系中，这行动属于每一个个体自身，只是发生在他今生的生活与意识之外；在另一个体系中，这行动则处于人类时间性历史的范围以内，却只是一个人的行动。如果说在奥利金的体系中，最令人反感的莫过于灵魂先存与堕落的观念，因为它似乎把异教观念引进了基督教信仰，那么在奥古斯丁的体系中，也同样越过了个人的生活与意识，企图用过去的一次行动解释人类现今的罪恶状态；不过，异教的柏拉图主义观点被换成了取自旧约的观点……因此，奥古斯丁的体系与奥利金的体系，本质上的区别仅在于：以亚当的堕落代替灵魂在时间之前的堕落；在奥利金那里仍披着异教外衣的东西，到了奥古斯丁那里便穿上了纯粹旧约的形式。”

奥古斯丁的学识不及他的天才，也没有奥利金和优西比乌 (Eusebius) 那样广博，但就他的时代而言，仍相当可观；在拉丁教父中，除哲罗姆一人以外，无人能及。他在马道拉 (Madaura) 和迦太基 (Carthage) 的学校里，为日后从事法庭辩论而接受了良好的理论与修辞训练，这对他的神学工作也大有裨益。他熟悉拉丁文学，绝非看不见古典作品的优点，尽管他认为这些作品远远不及圣经更高的美。大学求学期间，西塞罗 (Cicero) 的《霍尔滕西乌斯》(Hortensius，此书已佚) 激起了他对哲学的热忱，也激起了他为真理本身而求知的热忱；研读柏拉图主义和新柏拉图主义著作（修辞学家维克托里努斯 (Victorinus) 的拉丁文译本），又在他里面燃起了不可思议的烈火；271 然而，在这两类著作中，他都找不到耶稣的圣名，也找不到爱与谦卑这两种首要美德；他从中看见的只是美丽的理想，却没有力量使自己符合这些理想。他的《上帝之城》(City of God)、论异端的著作及其他作品，表明他广泛通晓古代哲学、诗歌，以及神圣与世俗的历史。他提到希腊和罗马最杰出的人物；他常常论及毕达哥拉斯 (Pythagoras)、柏拉图 (Plato)、亚里士多德 (Aristotle)、普罗提诺 (Plotin)、波菲利 (Porphyry)、西塞罗、塞涅卡、贺拉斯 (Horace)、维吉尔 (Virgil)，以及较早的希腊和拉丁教父、东方和西方的异端人物。但他对希腊文学的知识，大多得自拉丁文译本。至于希腊语，正如他自己坦率而谦逊地承认的，与哲罗姆相比，他只有粗浅的认识。272 希伯来语他则全然不懂。因此，尽管他对拉丁文圣经异常熟悉，在释经上仍犯了许多错误。与其说他是一位学者，不如说他是一位思想家；他主要依靠自身的才智，而这才智始终丰沛。216273

§ 179. 奥古斯丁的著作

奥古斯丁的众多著作，写作时间绵延四十四年，是基督教知识与经验的一座宝库。其中充满崇高的思想、高尚的情操、虔敬的倾诉、对真理的清晰陈述、驳斥谬误的有力论证，以及热情洋溢、具有不朽之美的雄辩篇章；但也充斥着无数重复、奇想异说，以及他那异常丰饶的头脑所作的游戏式猜测。274 他的文风充满生气与力量，善用巧妙的双关语，却欠缺纯净与典雅，也绝非没有令人厌倦的冗长，以及他那善于论辩的对手、埃克拉努姆的尤利安 (Julian of Eclanum) 所指责的那种 vagabunda loquacitas（漫无边际的饶舌）。他曾说，他宁可受到语法家的责备，也不愿百姓听不明白；他不大留心文体，尽管他的笔触屡屡如诗一般高飞。他不以文学声名为务，而是受爱上帝、爱教会之心的驱策，从充盈的心智与心灵中写作。他归信之前的著作，包括论美的《论美与适宜》(De Pulchro et Apto)，以及他在迦太基、罗马和米兰 (Milan) 担任修辞教师时发表的演说和颂词，都已失传。对于他身为雄辩术教授、异教哲学家、摩尼教异端信徒、怀疑论者和自由思想者的那些经历，我们只能从他在《忏悔录》及其他著作中的悔恨与自我纠正中得知。就我们所知，他的著述生涯始于他在卡西基亚库姆 (Cassiciacum) 的虔敬退隐生活；在那里，他为公开承认自己的信仰作准备。在卡西基亚库姆、罗马以及塔加斯特 (Tagaste) 附近写成的著作中，他起初以基督教哲学家的面貌出现；受按立为长老之后，则以神学家的面貌出现。然而，即使在神学著作中，他也处处显出其心智的形而上学与思辨倾向。他从未放弃或贬低理性，只是使理性服从信仰，并使它服务于启示真理的辩护。信仰是理性的先驱，先发现那片由理性加以探索的疆域。

以下将他最重要的著作分类概述，其中大部分的内容，我们已在前面各节中述及。275

I. 自传性著作。属于这一类的有《忏悔录》(Confessions) 和《订正录》(Retractations)；前者承认自己的罪，后者承认自己理论上的错误。在前者中，他严格审察自己的生活；在后者中，则严格审察自己的著作；因此，这两部作品为评判他的全部工作提供了最佳尺度。276

《忏悔录》是他笔下最有益、至少是最能造就人的作品；事实上，我们无疑可以说，这是全部教父文献中最能造就人的一本书。因此，即使在他生前，这也是他的著作中读者最多的一部，277 后来也成为再版最频繁的一部。278 从来没有一本书写得比它更真诚、更恳切。直到第十卷的历史叙述部分，是各信条群体共同珍视的灵修经典，其流行程度仅次于托马斯·肯皮斯 (Thomas a Kempis) 的《效法基督》(Imitation of Christ) 和班扬 (Bunyan) 的《天路历程》(Pilgrim’s Progress)。论真正的谦卑、属灵的深度和普遍的感染力，确实没有一部自传能胜过它。奥古斯丁曾是纵欲的异教徒、摩尼教异端信徒、焦灼的求道者、真诚的悔罪者，最后成为心怀感恩的归信者；凡在本性的诱惑和谬误的迷宫中挣扎，终于认识真理、看见圣洁之美，并在许多叹息与眼泪之后，于慈悲救主的怀抱中找到安息与平安的人，都能在自己的灵魂里映照出他的经历。卢梭 (Rousseau) 的《忏悔录》(Confessions) 和歌德 (Goethe) 的《诗与真》(Truth and Poetry)，虽然写作精神截然不同，但就其罕见的天才和引人入胜的魅力而言，仍可与奥古斯丁的《忏悔录》相比；然而，它们试图抬高未经成圣的人性，结果却越发暴露了人性的虚荣与软弱；而这位希坡 (Hippo) 主教的著作，因专一着眼于上帝的荣耀，却将人从悔罪的尘土中提升，进入圣灵所赐崭新而不朽的生命。279

奥古斯丁约在400年写成《忏悔录》。前十卷以在上帝面前不断祈祷和认罪的形式，概述了他早年的生活、归信，以及他在三十四岁时返回非洲的经过。这些卷中最突出的部分，是他在米兰归信的动人历程，以及他那高尚的母亲在奥斯提亚 (Ostia) 度过最后日子的记述；那些日子仿佛是在天堂的门口度过的，并且满怀确据，相信将来必在荣耀的宝座前蒙福重聚。最后三卷（以及第十卷的一部分）专论思辨哲学；它们探讨认识上帝的可能性以及时间与空间的本质等形而上学问题，部分内容暗中针对摩尼教 (Manichaeism)；它们也解释摩西所记的宇宙起源，采用教父惯用、却与我们时代格格不入的那种典型寓意释经方式。因此，对一般读者而言，这几卷价值不大，只是表明：即使抽象的形而上学题目，也可以用虔敬的方式加以论述。

《订正录》写于他生命的晚年（427年）。当时，他铭记“多言多语难免有过”这句箴言，280 也记得我们所说的每一句闲话，将来都必须交账，281 因而自我审判，免得受审判。282 他按时间顺序，重新审阅了自己任主教之前及任内写成的众多著作，凡在他更成熟的认识看来有错误或晦涩之处，都予以撤回或订正。然而，在一切根本要点上，他的神学体系从归信之时直到此时，始终未变。《订正录》为他的爱慕真理、认真尽责和谦卑，提供了美好的明证。283

奥古斯丁的《书信集》(Letters) 也应归入这一类。本笃会 (Benedictine) 编者在其第二卷中，按年代次序收录了公元386年至429年的二百七十封书信（包括写给奥古斯丁的信）。这些书信论及他那个时代的一切重要问题，有时讨论得极其细致，使我们得以窥见他的忧虑、他对职分的忠诚、他宽广的胸怀，以及他效法保罗、向什么样的人就作什么样的人的努力。

当朋友和学生提出的问题积累起来时，他便另撰专著加以答复；由此产生了多种《问题》(Quaestiones) 与《答复》(Responsiones) 集，内容涉及教义、释经及其他杂项（公元390年、397年等）。

  1. 以对话体写成的哲学论著；几乎全都作于他早年：有的是在他住于米兰附近的卡西基亚库姆乡间别墅期间写成的；受洗之前，他在那里度过半年，与莫尼卡 (Monica)、儿子阿德奥达图斯 (Adeodatus)、兄弟纳维吉乌斯 (Navigius)、朋友阿利皮乌斯 (Alypius)，以及几位表亲和学生，进行富有教益、启发心智的交谈，仿佛置身于一所学园，或一场基督教的柏拉图式宴饮之中；有的是在他第二次居留罗马期间写成的；还有一些则写于他返回非洲之后不久。284

属于这一类的著作有：《驳学园派》三卷 (Contra Academicos libri tres，386年)，其中他驳斥新学园派 (New Academy) 的怀疑主义和或然主义，即认为人永远不能达到真理，至多只能达到或然性的学说；《论幸福生活》(De vita beata，386年)，其中他认为真正的福乐在于完全认识上帝；《论秩序》(De ordine)，论恶与世界的神圣秩序之间的关系285（386年）；《独语录》(Soliloquia，387年)，是他与自己灵魂的交谈，论及上帝、至善、真理的知识和不朽；《论灵魂不朽》(De immortalitate animae，387年)，为《独语录》的续篇；《论灵魂的大小》(De quantitate animae，387年)，讨论灵魂的大小、起源及非物质性等种种问题；《论音乐》六卷 (De musica libri vi，387389年)；《论教师》(De magistro，389年)，其中他与儿子阿德奥达图斯对话，论述上帝之道的重要性与功效，以及基督作为无误的教师；阿德奥达图斯是个虔诚而有前途、但早熟的青年，返回非洲后不久便去世了（389年）。286 此外还可加上他后期的著作《论灵魂及其起源》(De anima et ejus origine，419年)。其他论语法、辩证法（或 ars bene disputandi，善于辩论之术）、修辞、几何及算术的哲学著作，均已散佚。287

这些著作所呈现的、具有鲜明基督教和教会特征的内容尚且不多；但其中展现的是一种被基督教精神所把握并圣化的柏拉图主义 (Platonism)，充满崇高的思想、理想的见解和精细的论证。这些著作旨在呈现他本人藉以达到真理知识的人类思想的各个阶段，也为别人提供通往圣所的阶梯。它们构成了他神学的初步导论。后来，他在《订正录》中撤回了其中的许多观点，例如柏拉图关于灵魂先存的看法，以及认为知识的获得乃是对心中隐藏的知识加以回忆或发掘的观念。288 此后，他身上的哲学家越来越让位于神学家，他的见解变得更加确定，也更注重经验，尽管在某些方面也更为狭窄、更为排他。然而，他始终不能停止哲学思考，甚至他后期的著作，尤其《论三位一体》(De Trinitate) 和《上帝之城》(De Civitate Dei)，也充满深邃的思辨。归信之前，他遵循某一特定的哲学体系，先是摩尼教的，后是柏拉图派的；归信之后，他接受了基督教哲学，这哲学以上帝在圣经中的启示为基础，是神学与宗教的婢女；但与此同时，他也为大公教会的教会哲学预备了道路，这种哲学以教会的权威为依据，并在中世纪的经院哲学中臻于完备。

在哲学史上，他理当位居最高之列；对于这门众学之学，他所作的贡献超过任何其他教父，连亚历山大的革利免 (Clement of Alexandria) 和俄利根 (Origen) 也不例外。他抨击并驳斥异教哲学，指出它在根本上不是泛神论，就是二元论；他动摇了占星术和魔法的迷信；他从哲学中驱除了流溢说，以及认为上帝是世界灵魂的观念；他大大推进了心理学；对于恶的起源与本质这一问题，他比所有前人都更接近解答，也不逊于大多数后人；他第一个彻底探究上帝的全能与全知同人的自由之间的关系，并构建神义论 (theodicy)；简言之，他确实是基督教哲学的奠基者，不仅与亚里士多德 (Aristotle) 平分了中世纪经院哲学的天下，也为新的哲学体系提供了富有生命力的萌芽；在以思辨奠立基督教教义时，人们将永远向他求教。

  1. 针对异教徒和犹太人的护教著作。其中，《上帝之城》(De Civitate Dei) 二十二卷至今仍十分值得一读。它是古代最深刻、最丰富的护教著作；始作于413年，即哥特王阿拉里克 (Alaric) 占领罗马之后，完成于426年，且经常单独出版。它浓缩了他关于世界与人的全部理论，也是第一次尝试以两股彼此对抗的潮流或有组织力量的二元视角，建立一套全面的世界历史哲学：一方是注定最终毁灭的今世之国，另一方则是永远长存的上帝之国。289

  2. 一般性的宗教—神学著作（部分针对摩尼教）：《论信仰的益处》(De utilitate credendi)，反对诺斯替派 (Gnostic) 对知识的高举（392年）；《论信仰与信经》(De fide et symbolo)，是他在393年应众主教之请，于希波 (Hippo) 会议上所作的一篇关于《使徒信经》的演说，当时他还只是一名长老；《论基督教教义》四卷 (De doctrina Christiana iv libri，397年；第四卷增补于426年)，是一部释经神学纲要，旨在教导人按照信仰的准则解释圣经；《论初信者的教导》(De catechizandis rudibus)，同样以教理问答的教导为目的（400年）；《手册》(Enchiridion)，又名《论信、望、爱》(De fide, spe et caritate)，是一部信仰与道德教义的简要纲要，由他于421年或更晚应劳伦提乌斯 (Laurentius) 之请写成，因此又称《致劳伦提乌斯手册》(Manuale ad Laurentium)。

V. 论战性的神学著作。这些是教义史最丰富的资料来源。《致库德武尔特德乌姆论异端》(De haeresibus ad Quodvultdeum) 一书对各异端作了总体考察；此书写于428至430年之间，是写给迦太基 (Carthage) 的一位友人兼执事的，概述了从西门派 (Simonians) 到伯拉纠派 (Pelagians) 的八十八种异端。290 在《论真宗教》(De vera religione，390年) 中，奥古斯丁旨在表明，真正的宗教不在异端者和分裂者中间，而只在当时的大公教会之中。

其他论战著作则分别针对摩尼教 (Manichaeism)、多纳徒派 (Donatism)、阿里乌派 (Arianism)、伯拉纠主义 (Pelagianism) 和半伯拉纠主义 (Semi-Pelagianism) 等特定异端。奥古斯丁虽然信念坚定，却不怀个人怨憎，而是以真正的基督徒精神执笔论战，fortiter in re, suaviter in modo（立场坚定，态度温和）。他领会了保罗所说的 ἀληθεύειν ἐν ἀγάπῃ*，在这方面与哲罗姆形成了令人欣慰的对照；哲罗姆天性的急烈大概并不超过他，却较不能自制。他对摩尼教徒说过一段十分优美的话：“让那些不知道寻求真理要付出多少辛劳、不知道防备谬误何等艰难的人，对你们燃起仇恨吧；至于我，经历了如此重大而长久的摇摆之后，才认识真理，就必须以同样的忍耐对待你们，正如我还在你们的见解中盲目狂乱地徘徊时，同信的人对待我那样。”291

  1. 反摩尼教著作大多写于他早年，在时间和内容上都紧接着他的哲学著作。292 后来，他在这些著作中发现需要撤回的地方最多，因为他曾主张意志自由，以反对摩尼教的宿命论。其中最重要的有：《论大公教会的道德与摩尼教徒的道德》(De moribus ecclesiae catholicae, et de moribus Manichaeorum)，共两卷（写于他第二次居留罗马期间，388年）；《论真宗教》(De vera religione，390年)；《恶从何来及论自由意志》(Unde malum, et de libero arbitrio)，通常简称《论自由意志》(De libero arbitrio)，共三卷，反对摩尼教认为恶是一种实体、其所在是物质而非自由意志的学说（始作于388年，完成于395年）；《驳摩尼教徒论创世记》(De Genesi contra Manichaeos)，为圣经的创造教义辩护（389年）；《论两个灵魂》(De duabus animabus)，反对摩尼教的心理二元论（392年）；《与福尔图纳图斯辩论》(Disputatio contra Fortunatum)，记述392年8月在希波对这位摩尼教祭司的成功驳斥；《驳摩尼所谓〈根本书信〉》(Contra Epistolam Manichaei quam vocant fundamenti，397年)；《驳摩尼教徒福斯图斯》(Contra Faustum Manichaeum)，共三十三卷（400—404年）；《论善的本性》(De natura boni，404年)，等等。

这些著作论述恶的起源、自由意志、旧约与新约以及启示与自然的和谐、与二元论和物活论 (hylozoism) 相对立的从无创造、信仰高于知识、圣经与教会的权威、真伪禁欲主义，以及其他有争议的问题；它们是我们认识摩尼教的诺斯替主义及反对它的论证的主要资料来源。奥古斯丁本人曾有九年属于这一教派，因此更适于承担驳斥它的任务，正如保罗特别适于驳斥法利赛派的犹太教一样。他关于恶的本质的教义尤其可贵。他以无可辩驳、足以垂诸永久的论证表明：恶不是有形体之物，也绝非任何实体，而是受造物自由意志的产物，是本身为善的实体遭到扭曲，是上帝所造本性的败坏。

  1. 针对普里西利安派 (Priscillianists)——一个在西班牙以摩尼教原理为基础的教派——而写的，有《致保罗·奥罗修斯驳普里西利安派与俄利根派》(Ad Paulum Orosium contra Priscillianistas et Origenistas，411年)；293 致孔森提乌斯 (Consentius) 的《驳说谎》(Contra mendacium，420年)；还有第190封书信（另一编号为 Ep. 157）的部分内容，此信写于418年，是致奥普塔图斯 (Optatus) 主教论灵魂起源的；另有两封书信，他在其中驳斥了关于灵魂本性、来世刑罚有限，以及为自以为良善的目的而行欺诈乃属正当的错误见解。

  2. 反多纳徒派著作写于393至420年之间，旨在反对分离主义，其中包含奥古斯丁关于教会、教会纪律和圣礼的教义。属于这一类的有：《反多纳徒派诗篇》(Psalmus contra partem Donati，公元393年)，是一首没有规则格律的通俗论战歌曲，旨在抵消多纳徒派歌曲的影响；《驳帕尔梅尼安书信》(Contra epistolam Parmeniani)，写于400年，针对多纳徒 (Donatus) 的继任者、多纳徒派的迦太基主教；《驳多纳徒派论洗礼》(De baptismo contra Donatistas)，主张异端所施洗礼的有效性（400年）；《驳佩提利安书信》(Contra literas Petiliani，约400年)，反对居普良 (Cyprian) 和多纳徒派认为圣礼的效力取决于施礼祭司个人的配得程度及其教会身份的观点；《致大公教徒驳多纳徒派书》(Ad Catholicos Epistola contra Donatistas)，通称《论教会的合一》(De unitate ecclesiae，402年)；《驳多纳徒派语法学家克雷斯科尼乌斯》(Contra Cresconium grammaticum Donatistam，406年)；《与多纳徒派会谈简记》(Breviculus collationis cum Donatistis)，简述与多纳徒派为期三天的宗教会议（411年）；《论矫正多纳徒派》(De correctione Donatistarum，417年)；《驳多纳徒派主教高登提乌斯》(Contra Gaudentium, Donat. Episcopum)，为最后一部反多纳徒派著作（420年）。294

  3. 反阿里乌派著作涉及基督与圣灵的神性，以及神圣的三位一体。其中最重要的，远非其他著作可比，乃是《论三位一体》十五卷 (De Trinitate，400—416年)；这是古代教会论三位一体最深刻、辨析最精细的著作，丝毫不逊于亚他那修 (Athanasius) 和两位贵格利 (Gregories) 的同类作品，并在数百年间成为这一教义的定论。295 此书也可归入正面教导性的著作，因为它并非直接的论战之作。《与阿里乌派马克西米努斯会谈》(Collatio cum Maximino Ariano) 则写于428年；此人是个名不见经传、喋喋不休之徒。

  4. 奥古斯丁众多反伯拉纠派的著作，是他最有影响、也最有价值的著作。它们写于412至429年之间。在这些著作中，正值思想与灵性鼎盛时期的奥古斯丁，阐发了他的人论和救恩论体系，并且最为接近福音派新教的立场：《论罪责、赦罪与婴儿洗礼》（412年）；《论精意与字句》（413年）；《论本性与恩典》（415年）；《论伯拉纠案的议事录》（417年）；《论基督的恩典与原罪》（418年）；《论婚姻与情欲》（419年）；《论恩典与自由意志》（426年）；《论管教与恩典》（427年）；《驳埃克拉努姆的尤利安》〔Julian of Eclanum；两部大作，写于421至429年之间，第二部未完成，因而称为《未完成之作》(Opus imperfectum)〕；《论圣徒的预定》（428年）；《论恒忍的恩赐》（429年）；等等。296

  1. 释经著作。其中最优秀的有：《创世记字义解》(De Genesi ad literam，即逐字解释《创世记》)，十二卷，详解《创世记》前三章，尤其按字义解释创造的历史，但也包含许多奥秘性和寓意性的解释（写于401至415年）；297《诗篇释义》(Enarrationes in Psalmos，大部分为讲道)；298一百二十四篇《约翰福音讲道》（416和417年）；299十篇《约翰一书讲道》（417年）；《登山宝训释义》（393年）；《福音书的和谐》(De consensu evangelistarum，400年)；《加拉太书释义》（394年）；以及未完成的《罗马书注释》。300

奥古斯丁的释经，与其说是严格的语法和历史解释，不如说是对圣经所作的生动、深刻而有益于造就的思考；对于前一种解释，无论他还是他的读者，都缺乏必要的语言知识、意向和趣味。他的神学，与其说建立在释经之上，不如说建立在他那浸透圣经真理、具有基督徒和教会精神的心思之上。

  1. 伦理性或实践性及禁欲著作。这一类包括三百九十六篇《讲道》(Sermones，大多很短)，分为 de Scripturis（讲解圣经经文）、de tempore（节期讲道）、de sanctis（纪念使徒、殉道者和圣徒）以及 de diversis（适用于各种场合）；其中有些由奥古斯丁口授，有些由听众记录。301此外还有各种道德论著：《论节制》(De continentia，395年)；《论说谎》(De mendacio，395年)，反对欺骗（不可与前面已提到、写于420年、反对普里西利安派欺诈理论的同类著作《驳说谎》〔Contra mendacium〕混淆）；《论基督徒的争战》(De agone Christiano，396年)；《论修士的劳作》(De opere monachorum，400年)，反对修道生活中的懒惰；《驳约维尼安论婚姻之善》(De bono conjugali adv. Jovinianum，400年)；《论童贞》(De virginitate，401年)；《论信心与行为》(De fide et operibus，413年)；《论奸淫的婚姻》(De adulterinis conjugiis，419年)，论《哥林多前书》七章10节及下文；《论守寡之善》(De bono viduitatis，418年)；《论忍耐》(De patientia，418年)；《论为死者应有的照料》(De cura pro mortuis gerenda，421年)，致诺拉的保利努斯 (Paulinus of Nola)；《论禁食的益处》(De utilitate jejunii)；《论爱上帝》(De diligendo Deo)；《默想录》(Meditationes)；等等。302

当我们纵览这浩繁的著述工作，再加上许多如今已经散佚的论著和书信，并想到他的主教职务、频繁的旅行，以及他为信徒裁断争端、往往耗去整日的事务时，我们实在不能不惊叹于这位教父的忠诚、丰沛的才思、精力和坚忍。诚然，这样的一生是不虚度的一生。

§ 180. 奥古斯丁对后世的影响及其与天主教和新教的关系

在我们告别这位令人肃然起敬的人物，以及他在其中如最明亮之星一般闪耀的那段教会历史之前，必须就奥古斯丁对其后世界的影响，以及他在天主教与新教这一重大对立中的地位，再作一些观察。诚然，所有教父都是双方共同的遗产；但没有哪一位像奥古斯丁那样，对双方产生如此持久的影响，也没有哪一位如此受到双方的尊崇。惟独对希腊教会，他的影响甚微，甚或全无；因为这一教会停留在前一时期尚未充分发展的人神协作论 (synergistic) 的人论上。303

  1. 首先，奥古斯丁对于天主教与新教共同持守、用以抵挡摩尼教 (Manichaeism)、阿里乌主义 (Arianism) 和伯拉纠主义 (Pelagianism) 等古代根本性异端的教义基础，其发展贡献甚大。在所有这些重大的思想交锋中，他大体上都是基督教真理的捍卫者，抵挡种种危险的谬误。在他的影响下，希波 (Hippo，393年) 和迦太基 (Carthage，397年) 的会议确定了圣经正典现有的形式（其中诚然也包括旧约次经）。他战胜了摩尼教的二元论、物活论 (hylozoism) 和宿命论，保全了圣经关于上帝和创造的观念，以及关于罪的本质和罪源于人自由意志的教义。他发展了尼西亚 (Nicene) 的三位一体教义，以圣灵双重发出的教义使之完备，并赋予它一种此后一直通行于西方的形式；他的学派在《亚他那修信经》(Athanasian Creed) 中，使这一形式获得了经典的表达。反之，在基督论上，他没有增添什么；他去世后不久，重大的基督论争端才开始，而这些争端在他死后二十年，于迦克墩会议 (Council of Chalcedon) 获得了普世性的解决。然而，他先于利奥 (Leo)，使“一位格中有二性”这一重要公式通行于西方。304

  2. 奥古斯丁也是拉丁天主教体系在神学上的主要创立者；这一体系一方面有别于希腊天主教，另一方面有别于福音派新教。他支配了整个中世纪的神学，凭借其善于辩证的心智，成为经院哲学之父，又凭借其虔敬的心灵，成为神秘主义之父，却不应为这两种体系各自的过度发展负责。因为经院哲学自以为能用理智理解神圣的事物，最终迷失于空洞的辩证之中；神秘主义力图以情感把握神圣的事物，很容易流入朦胧的感伤主义；奥古斯丁则寻求以心智与心灵、大胆的思想与谦卑的信仰相结合的力量，来领会神圣的事物。305在这方面，安瑟伦 (Anselm)、克莱尔沃的伯尔纳 (Bernard of Clairvaux)、托马斯·阿奎那 (Thomas Aquinas) 和波纳文图拉 (Bonaventura)，与他的关系最为密切。即便如今，大公教会已经成为罗马教会，他在其中受到的尊崇，仍超过安布罗斯 (Ambrose)、希拉里 (Hilary)、哲罗姆或大贵格利 (Gregory the Great)。若没有内在的亲缘性，这一切绝不可能得到解释。306

就连他的归信本身——在这件事上，除了圣经之外，与维护圣统制的安布罗斯的亲身交往，以及苦修者安东尼 (Anthony) 的生平，都产生了重大影响——也不是从异教转向基督教（因为他已经是一个摩尼教基督徒），而是从异端转向那历史传承、按主教制组织起来的教会；在当时，这教会乃是使徒基督教唯一获认可的承载者，与那些或多或少攻击福音根基的宗派和党派相抗衡。307这诚然是将他的心智与心灵完全而无条件地降服于上帝，但同时也是将个人判断顺服于那引导他接受福音信仰的教会权威。308他也以同样的精神接受了禁欲生活；按照天主教的原则，没有这种生活，就不可能有崇高的宗教生活。他固然没有像路德 (Luther) 那样进入修道院——路德在爱尔福特 (Erfurt) 的归信，其本质同样是天主教式的——却在自己家中过着修士般简朴的生活，并立下且持守自愿贫穷和独身的誓愿。309

他接受了居普良的教会论，并在与多纳徒主义的争论中使之完备，将合一、圣洁、普世、排他和母亲身份这些属性，直接赋予当时现实中的教会；这教会具有稳固的主教制组织、不间断的传承和《使徒信经》，胜利地抵挡了当时异端名录中八十个或一百个敌对宗派，并以罗马为其有形的中心。在这教会中，他从人生的覆舟中获得拯救，找到了真正基督教的家园、思想的坚实基础、心灵的满足，以及足以施展其广泛才能的相称领域。惟独无误性这一属性，他没有明确提出；他认为，后来的会议会逐步纠正早期的会议；在伯拉纠争论中，他对教皇佐西穆 (Zosimus) 表现出的独立性，与先前居普良在异端洗礼争论中对教皇司提反 (Stephen) 所表现的一样，而他的有利之处在于真理站在他这一边，以致佐西穆不得不向非洲教会让步。310

他首先为圣礼作出了明确而固定的定义：圣礼是基于上帝设立、表明无形恩典的有形记号；但他全然不知道圣礼有七件，这乃是很久以后才作出的规定。在洗礼的教义上，他完全是天主教的，311尽管这在逻辑上与他的预定论相矛盾；但在圣餐的教义上，他与其前辈德尔图良和居普良一样，更接近加尔文派 (Calvinistic) 关于基督身体和宝血属灵临在、并为信徒享用的理论。至少在他后期的著作中，他也促进了天主教对神迹的信仰312和对马利亚的崇拜；313不过，他只认为童贞女免于本罪，而非免于原罪；尽管对她极其尊敬，他却从未称她为上帝之母。314

他起初主张宗教自由，主张纯粹以属灵的方法反对谬误，后来却倡导了“勉强人进来”这一祸患深重的原则（coge intrare），并以其权威的巨大分量，支持世俗政权施行逼迫的制度；这一制度在中世纪结出的血腥果实，连他自己见了也会战栗；因为他内心始终是一个仁爱温柔的人，并且亲自实践这一崇高的原则：“惟有真理能够得胜，而真理的胜利就是爱。”315

由此可见，即使是真正伟大而良善的人，也会因误用热忱，无意间成为许多祸害的始作俑者。

  1. 然而，另一方面，在所有教父中，奥古斯丁最接近福音派新教；就其罪与恩典的教义而言，可以称他为宗教改革的第一位先驱。路德宗和改革宗教会向来毫无疑虑地承认他“圣徒”的称号，并认他为最明达的真理见证人之一，也是神圣恩典改变罪人之奇妙能力最显著的例证之一。值得注意的是，他那些与新教最为接近的保罗式教义，乃是他体系中较晚、较成熟的部分，而恰恰是这些教义在平信徒中广受欢迎。他在人论上有所发展的伯拉纠争论，标志着其神学与教会事业的顶峰；他最后的著作所针对的，是伯拉纠派的尤利安 (Julian)，以及高卢 (Gaul) 的半伯拉纠派 (Semi-Pelagians)；后者是由两位友好的平信徒普罗斯珀 (Prosper) 和希拉里提请他注意的。这些反伯拉纠派著作，确实曾强有力地影响天主教会，并抑制了圣统制和修道制度中趋向伯拉纠主义的倾向，但它们从未深入这教会的血肉骨髓。它们等待着一个有利的未来，并在沉默中滋养着对现行体系的反抗。

即使在中世纪，那些试图通过回归圣经，使占统治地位的基督教简化、洁净并属灵化的较优良宗派，以及宗教改革以前的改革者，如威克里夫 (Wiclif)、鲁斯 (Russ)、韦塞尔 (Wessel)，在使徒保罗之外，也最多求助于这位希波主教，视他为白白恩典之教义的代表。

宗教改革者受他的著作引导，对保罗有了更深的理解，从而为他们伟大的使命作了准备。没有一位教会教师像他那样深刻地塑造了路德和加尔文 (Calvin)；没有一位为他们提供了如此有力的武器，用以反对占统治地位的伯拉纠主义和形式主义；也没有一位如此频繁地被他们怀着敬重和爱戴加以引用。316

所有宗教改革者起初都接受了他对自由意志的否定及其预定论，梅兰希顿 (Melancthon) 和慈运理 (Zwingle) 也在其中；他们有时甚至比他走得更远，深入堕落前预定论 (supralapsarianism) 的深渊，以铲除人类功德与自夸的最后根苗。在这一点上，奥古斯丁之于天主教会，正如路德之于路德宗教会：他是一位权威无可非议的异端者，即使在其偏激之处，所受的赞赏也多于责难；然而，他的预定论却在詹森主义 (Jansenism) 受教皇谴责时，间接地受到了谴责，正如路德的观点被《协同信条》当作加尔文主义而加以拒绝一样。317因为詹森主义无非是奥古斯丁主义在罗马天主教会内部的复兴。318

奥古斯丁和宗教改革者在这一方向上的过度，源于他们对罪与恩典的认识是如此恳切而强烈。伯拉纠主义的松弛态度绝不可能孕育出一位改革者。唯有坚定不移地确信人自身的无能，确信人对上帝无条件的倚赖，确信上帝的恩典有全能，能赐我们力量行各样善事，才可能造就这样的人。一个人若要使别人确信，他领受了为教会和人类效力的神圣呼召，他自己就必须深信上帝那永恒不变的旨意，并且在最黑暗的时刻仍紧紧持守这一信念。

重大的对立面和看似相互冲突的真理，能够共存于伟人之中，也唯有伟人才能容纳。狭小的心智容纳不了它们。大公的、教会性的、圣礼性的和祭司性的体系，与注重主观个人经验的福音派新教基督教彼此冲突。尤其是普遍的洗礼重生教义，它以普遍的呼召为前提（至少在教会内部如此），按逻辑原则，很难与绝对预定论相调和；后者把救赎的旨意限于受洗者中的一部分人。奥古斯丁一方面认为，每一个受洗者都借着伴随圣礼外在行动的圣灵内在运行，领受罪的赦免，从自然状态迁入恩典状态，因而，qua baptizatus（就其受洗者的身份而言），也是上帝的儿女和永生的后嗣；但另一方面，他又使这一切福分都取决于上帝绝对的旨意；上帝只从“沉沦的群体”中拯救一定数目的人，并保守他们到底。在他看来，重生与拣选并不像在加尔文那里那样彼此重合。前者可以存在而没有后者，后者却不能存在而没有前者。奥古斯丁认为，许多人确实重生进入了恩典的国，却只是为了再次灭亡；加尔文则认为，对未蒙拣选的人而言，洗礼是一个毫无意义的仪式；前者把幻象置于内在功效中，后者则把它置于外在形式中。圣礼性的、教会性的体系把重点放在洗礼重生上，以致损及永恒的拣选；加尔文主义和清教徒的体系则为拣选而牺牲圣礼的功效；路德宗和圣公会的体系试图寻求中间立场，却无法在神学上为这一问题提供令人满意的解决。圣公会容许这两种对立的观点，在《公祷书》的洗礼仪文中认可前一种，又在带有温和加尔文主义色彩的《三十九条信纲》中认可后一种。

奥古斯丁的体系与哲罗姆的拉丁文圣经一样，恰好出现在历史的过渡时期：古代文明正在野蛮势力的洪流面前消逝，一种新的秩序则在基督教的引导下酝酿形成。这显然是上帝的安排。教会必须凭借其强大而威严的组织，以及稳固的教义体系，在民族大迁徙的混乱动荡中保存基督教，并成为中世纪蛮族诸民族的教养学校。319

在这一教养过程中，除圣经之外，哲罗姆的学识，以及奥古斯丁的神学和丰富思想，乃是最重要的精神力量。

奥古斯丁受到如此普遍的敬重，以致他能够在各个方向发挥影响，甚至其过激之处也不致引起反感。他具有充分的大公性，足以支持教会权威的原则；同时，他又如此自由而富有福音精神，能够修正这一原则的圣统制与圣礼性质，抵制它流于外在、机械的仪式主义的倾向，并使深刻的罪与恩典意识，以及热切而真正属基督的敬虔精神，始终存留，直到这种精神强大到足以冲破圣统制监护的外壳，进入其发展的新阶段。在对中世纪天主教发挥有益影响，以及为福音派宗教改革作有效预备这两方面，没有哪位教父能够胜过圣奥古斯丁；他不愧为保罗的继承者，也是路德和加尔文的先驱。

假如他生活在宗教改革时代，他极有可能领导福音运动，反对罗马教会中盛行的伯拉纠主义。因为我们不可忘记，尽管大公教会传统 (Catholicism) 与罗马主义 (Romanism)，或教皇主义 (Popery)，关系密切，两者之间仍有重要区别。它们彼此的关系，类似于旧约体制下的犹太教与基督被钉十字架及耶路撒冷被毁之后的犹太教之间的关系：前者仰望基督教，并为之预备道路，后者却与基督教敌对。大公教会传统涵盖教会古代和中世纪的全部历史，其中也包括保罗式、奥古斯丁式或福音派的倾向；这些倾向随着教皇制度的腐败，以及人们日益意识到有必要进行“reformatio in capite et membris”（首脑与肢体的改革），而不断增强。严格意义上的罗马主义始于特伦托会议 (council of Trent)，该会议为它赋予了信条上的表述，并咒诅宗教改革的教义。大公教会传统是罗马主义的力量，罗马主义则是大公教会传统的弱点。大公教会传统产生了詹森主义，教皇主义却谴责了它。教皇主义从不遗忘，也从不学习；它不能容许教义有任何改变（增添除外），否则就要牺牲其无误性的根本原则，从而自取灭亡。但大公教会传统最终可能挣脱教皇主义长久加在它身上的锁链，获得新的生命和活力。

奥古斯丁这样的人物，至今仍在基督教世界两大分支之间居于中介地位，同受双方尊崇，对双方有同样的影响；这更为一个令人振奋的前景提供了可喜的凭据：将来大公教会传统与新教可能在更高的合一中和解，保全双方所有的真理，摒弃双方所有的错误，赦免双方所有的罪，忘却双方所有的仇怨。归根结底，宗教中权威与自由、客观与主观、教会性与个人性、有机整体与个体、圣礼与亲身经验之间的矛盾，并非绝对，而是相对且暂时的；它的产生，与其说源于事物的本性，不如说源于今世人类知识与敬虔的欠缺。这些要素可以在教会的完全状态中达到最终的和谐，与神性和人性的联合相对应；这种联合超越有限思想和逻辑理解的界限，却已在基督的位格中完全实现。事实上，这些要素在圣保罗的神学体系中已经联合起来；他对教会持有最高的看法，视其为奥秘的“基督的身体”，以及“真理的柱石和根基”，同时他又是福音自由、个人责任，以及信徒与救主亲自联合的伟大捍卫者。我们相信并盼望一个圣洁、大公、使徒性的教会，一个圣徒相通的团契，一个羊圈，一位牧人。各教会越是真正成为基督的教会，或越是亲近基督，越是切实彰显他的国度，就越会彼此亲近。因为基督是一切信徒共同的元首和生命的中心，也是一切不和的人间宗派与信条的神圣和谐。奥古斯丁最伟大、最高尚的门徒之一帕斯卡尔 (Pascal) 说：在基督里，在基督里，一切矛盾都得到解决。

Original Text

CHAPTER X. CHURCH FATHERS, AND THEOLOGICAL LITERATURE.

Comp. the general literature on the Fathers in vol. i. § 116, and the special literature in the several sections following.

I.—THE GREEK FATHERS.

§ 161. Eusebius of C sarea.

I. Eusebius Pamphili: Opera omnia Gr. et Lat., curis variorum nempe II. Valesii, Fr. Vigeri, B. Montfaucon, Card. Angelo Maii edita; collegit et denuo recognovit J. P. Migne. Par. (Petit-Montrouge) 1857. 6 vols. (tom. xix.-xxiv. of Migne’s Patrologia Graeca). Of his several works his Church History has been oftenest edited, sometimes by itself, sometimes in connection with his Vita Constantini, and with the church histories of his successors; best by Henr. Valesius (Du Valois), Par. 1659–’73, 8 vols., and Cantabr. 1720, 3 vols., and again 1746 (with additions by G. Reading, best ed.); also (without the later historians) by E. Zimmermann, Francof. 1822; F. A. Heinichen, Lips. 1827–’8, 3 vols.; E. Burton, Oxon. 1838, 2 vols. (1845 and 1856 in 1 vol.); Schwegler, Tüb. 1852; also in various translations: In German by Stroth, Quedlinburg, 1776 ff., 2 vols.; by Closs, Stuttg. 1839; and several times in French and English; in English by Hanmer (1584), T. Shorting, and better by Chr. Fr. Cruse (an Amer. Episcopalian of German descent, died in New York, 1865): The Ecclesiastical History of Euseb. Pamph., etc., Now York, 1856 (10th ed.), and Lond. 1858 (in Bohn’s Eccles. Library). Comp. also the literary notices in Brunet, sub Euseb., and James Darling, Cyclop. Bibliograph. p. 1072 ff.

  1. Biographies by Hieronymus (De viris illustr. c. 81, a brief sketch, with a list of his works), Valesius (De vita scriptisque Eusebii Caesar.), W. Cave (Lives of the most eminent Fathers of the Church, vol. ii. pp. 95–144, ed. H. Cary, Oxf. 1840), Heinichen, Stroth, Cruse, and others, in their editions of the Eccles. Hist. of Eusebius. F. C. Baur: Comparatur Eusebius Hist. eccl. parens cum parente Hist. Herodoto. Tub. 1834. Haenell: De Euseb. Caes. religionis christ. defensore. Gott. 1843. Sam. Lee: Introductory treatise in his Engl. edition of the Theophany of Eusebius, Cambr. 1843. Semisch: Art. Eusebius v. Caes. in Herzog’s Encycl. vol. iv. (1855), pp. 229–238. Lyman Coleman: Eusebius as an historian, in the Bibliotheca Sacra, Andover, 1858, pp. 78–96. (The biography by Acacius, his successor in the see of Caesarea, Socr. ii. 4, is lost.)

This third period is uncommonly rich in great teachers of the church, who happily united theological ability and practical piety, and who, by their development of the most important dogmas in conflict with mighty errors, earned the gratitude of posterity. They monopolized all the learning and eloquence of the declining Roman empire, and made it subservient to the cause of Christianity for the benefit of future generations. They are justly called fathers of the church; they belong to Christendom without distinction of denominations; and they still, especially Athanasius and Chrysostom among the Greek fathers, and Augustine and Jerome among the Latin, by their writings and their example, hold powerful sway, though with different degrees of authority according to the views entertained by the various churches concerning the supremacy of the Bible and the value of ecclesiastical tradition.

We begin the series of the most important Nicene and post-Nicene divines with Eusebius of Caesarea, the "father of church history," the Christian Herodotus.

He was born about the year 260 or 270, probably in Palestine, and was educated at Antioch, and afterwards at Caesarea in Palestine, under the influence of the works of Origen. He formed an intimate friendship with the learned presbyter Pamphilus,(1) who had collected a considerable biblical and patristic library, and conducted a flourishing theological school which he had founded at Caesarea, till in 309 he died a martyr in the persecution under Diocletian.(2) Eusebius taught for a long time in this school; and after the death of his preceptor and friend, he travelled to Tyre and to Egypt, and was an eye-witness of the cruel scenes of the last great persecution of the Christians. He was imprisoned as a confessor, but soon released.

Twenty years later, when Eusebius, presiding at the council at Tyre (335 or 336), took sides against Athanasius, the bishop Potamon of Hieraclea, according to the account of Epiphanius, exclaimed in his face: "How dost thou, Eusebius, sit as judge of the innocent Athanasius? Who can bear it? Why! didst thou not sit with me in prison in the time of the tyrants? They plucked out my eye for my confession of the truth; thou camest forth unhurt; thou hast suffered nothing for thy confession; unscathed thou art here present. How didst thou escape from prison? On some other ground than because thou didst promise to do an unlawful thing [to sacrifice to idols]? or, perchance, didst thou actually do this? "But this insinuation of cowardice and infidelity to Christ arose probably from envy and party passion in a moment of excitement. With such a stain upon him, Eusebius would hardly have been intrusted by the ancient church with the episcopal staff.(3)

About the year 315, or earlier, Eusebius was chosen bishop of Caesarea,(4) where he labored till his death in 340. The patriarchate of Antioch, which was conferred upon him after the deposition of Eustathius in 331, he in honorable self-denial, and from preference for a more quiet literary life, declined.

He was drawn into the Arian controversies against his will, and played an eminent part at the council of Nicaea, where he held the post of honor at the right hand of the presiding emperor. In the perplexities of this movement he took middle ground, and endeavored to unite the opposite parties. This brought him, on the one hand, the peculiar favor of the emperor Constantine, but, on the other, from the leaders of the Nicene orthodoxy, the suspicion of a secret leaning to the Arian heresy.189(5) It is certain that, before the council of Nicaea, he sympathized with Arius; that in the council he proposed an orthodox but indefinite compromise-creed; that after the council he was not friendly with Athanasius and other defenders of orthodoxy; and that, in the synod of Tyre, which deposed Athanasius in 335, he took a leading part, and, according to Epiphanius, presided. In keeping with these facts is his silence respecting the Arian controversy (which broke out in 318) in an Ecclesiastical History which comes down to 324, and was probably not completed till 326, when the council of Nicaea would have formed its most fitting close. He would rather close his history with the victory of Constantine over Licinius than with the Creed over which theological parties contended, and with which he himself was implicated. But, on the other hand, it is also a fact that he subscribed the Nicene Creed, though reluctantly, and reserving his own interpretation of the homoousion; that he publicly recommended it to the people of his diocese; and that he never formally rejected it.

The only satisfactory solution of this apparent inconsistency is to be found in his own indecision and leaning to a doctrinal latitudinarianism, not unfrequent in historians who become familiar with a vast variety of opinions in different ages and countries. On the important point of the homoousion he never came to a firm and final conviction. He wavered between the older Origenistic subordinationism and the Nicene orthodoxy. He asserted clearly and strongly with Origen the eternity of the Son, and so far was decidedly opposed to Arianism, which made Christ a creature in time; but he recoiled from the homoousion, because it seemed to him to go beyond the Scriptures, and hence he made no use of the term, either in his book against Marcellus, or in his discourses against Sabellius. Religious sentiment compelled him to acknowledge the full deity of Christ; fear of Sabellianism restrained him. He avoided the strictly orthodox formulas, and moved rather in the less definite terms of former times. Theological acumen he constitutionally lacked. He was, in fact, not a man of controversy, but of moderation and peace. He stood upon the border between the ante-Nicene theology and the Nicene. His doctrine shows the color of each by turns, and reflects the unsettled problem of the church in the first stage of the Arian controversy.(6)

With his theological indecision is connected his weakness of character. He was an amiable and pliant court-theologian, and suffered himself to be blinded and carried away by the splendor of the first Christian emperor, his patron and friend. Constantine took him often into his counsels, invited him to his table, related to him his vision of the cross, showed him the famous labarum, listened standing to his occasional sermons, wrote him several letters, and intrusted to him the supervision of the copies of the Bible for the use of the churches in Constantinople.

At the celebration of the thirtieth anniversary of this emperor’s reign (336), Eusebius delivered a panegyric decked with the most pompous hyperbole, and after his death, in literal obedience to the maxim: "De mortuis nihil nisi bonum," he glorified his virtues at the expense of veracity and with intentional omission of his faults. With all this, however, he had noble qualities of mind and heart, which in more quiet times would have been an ornament to any episcopal see. And it must be said, to his honor, that he never claimed the favor of the emperor for private ends.

The theological and literary value of Eusebius lies in the province of learning. He was an unwearied reader and collector, and probably surpassed all the other church fathers, hardly excepting even Origen and Jerome, in compass of knowledge and of acquaintance with Grecian literature both heathen and Christian; while in originality, vigor, sharpness, and copiousness of thought, he stands far below Origen, Athanasius, Basil, and the two Gregories. His scholarship goes much further in breadth than in depth, and is not controlled and systematized by a philosophical mind or a critical judgment.

Of his works, the historical are by far the most celebrated and the most valuable; to wit, his Ecclesiastical History, his Chronicle, his Life of Constantine, and a tract on the Martyrs of Palestine in the Diocletian persecution. The position of Eusebius, at the close of the period of persecution, and in the opening of the period of the imperial establishment of Christianity, and his employment of many ancient documents, some of which have since been lost, give these works a peculiar value. He is temperate, upon the whole, impartial, and truth-loving—rare virtues in an age of intense excitement and polemical zeal like that in which he lived. The fact that he was the first to work this important field of theological study, and for many centuries remained a model in it, justly entitles him to his honorable distinction of Father of Church History. Yet he is neither a critical student nor an elegant writer of history, but only a diligent and learned collector. His Ecclesiastical History, from the birth of Christ to the victory of Constantine over Licinius in 324, gives a colorless, defective, incoherent, fragmentary, yet interesting picture of the heroic youth of the church, and owes its incalculable value, not to the historic art of the author, but almost entirely to his copious and mostly literal extracts from foreign, and in some cases now extinct, sources. As concerns the first three centuries, too, it stands alone; for the successors of Eusebius begin their history where he leaves off.

His Chronicle consists of an outline-sketch of universal history down to 325, arranged by ages and nations (borrowed largely from the Chronography of Julius Africanus), and an abstract of this universal chronicle in tabular form. The Greek original is lost, with the exception of unconnected fragments by Syncellus; but the second part, containing the chronological tables, was translated and continued by Jerome to 378, and remained for centuries the source of the synchronistic knowledge of history, and the basis of historical works in Christendom.(7) Jerome also translated, with several corrections and additions, a useful antiquarian work of Eusebius, the so-called Onomasticon, a description of the places mentioned in the Bible.(8)

In his Life, and still more in his Eulogy, of Constantine, Eusebius has almost entirely forgotten the dignity of the historian in the zeal of the panegyrist. Nevertheless, this work is the chief source of the history of the reign of his imperial friend.(9)

Next in importance to his historical works are his apologetic; namely, his Praeparatio evangelica,(10) and his Demonstratio evangelica.(11) These were both written before 324, and are an arsenal of the apologetic material of the ancient church. The former proposes, in fifteen books, to give a documentary refutation of the heathen religious from Greek writings. The latter gives, in twenty books, of which only the first ten are preserved, the positive argument for the absolute truth of Christianity, from its nature, and from the fulfilment of the prophecies in the Old Testament. The Theophany, in five books, is a popular compend from these two works, and was probably written later, as Epiphanius wrote his Anacephalaeosis after the Panarion, for more general use.(12) It is known in the Greek original from fragments only, published by Cardinal Mai,(13) and now complete in a Syriac version which was discovered in 1839 by Tattam, in a Nitrian monastery, and was edited by Samuel Lee at London in 1842.(14) To this class also belongs his apologetic tract Against Hierocles.(15)

Of much less importance are the two dogmatic works of Eusebius: Against Marcellus, and Upon the Church Theology (likewise against Marcellus), in favor of the hypostatical existence of the Son.(16)

His Commentaries on several books of the Bible (Isaiah, Psalms, Luke) pursue, without independence, and without knowledge of the Hebrew, the allegorical method of Origen.(17)

To these are to be added, finally, some works in Biblical Introduction and Archaeology, the Onomasticon, already alluded to, a sort of sacred geography, and fragments of an enthusiastic Apology for Origen, a juvenile work which he and Pamphilus jointly produced before 309, and which, in the Origenistic controversy, was the target of the bitterest shots of Epiphanius and Jerome.(18)

§ 162. The Church Historians after Eusebius.

I. The Church Histories of Socrates, Sozomen, Theodoret, Evagrius, Philostorgius, and Theodorus Lector have been edited, with the Eccles. Hist. of Eusebius, by Valesius, Par. 1659–’73, in 3 vols. (defective reprint, Frankf. a. M. 1672–’79); best ed., Cambridge, 1720, and again 1746, in 3 vols., with improvements and additions by Guil. Reading. Best English translation by Meredith, Hanmer, and Wye Saltonstall, Cambr. 1688, 1692, and London, 1709. New ed. in Bohn’s Ecclesiastical Library, Land. 1851, in 4 vols. small 8vo.

  1. F. A. Holzhausen: De fontibus, quibus Socrates, Sozomenus, ac Theodoretus in scribenda historia sacra usi sunt. Gött. 1825. G. Dangers: De fontibus, indole et dignitate librorum Theod. Lectoris et Evagrii. Gött. 1841. J. G. Dowling: An Introduction to the Critical Study of Eccl. History. Lond. 1838, p. 84 ff. F. Chr. Baur: Die Epochen der kirchlichen Geschichtschreibung. Tüb. 1852, pp. 7–32. Comp. P. Schaff: History of the Apostolic Church, Gen. Introd. p. 52 f.

Eusebius, without intending it, founded a school of church historians, who continued the thread of his story from Constantine the Great to the close of the sixth century, and, like him, limited themselves to a simple, credulous narration of external facts, and a collection of valuable documents, without an inkling of the critical sifting, philosophical mastery, and artistic reproduction of material, which we find in Thucydides and Tacitus among the classics, and in many a modern historian. None of them touched the history of the first three centuries; Eusebius was supposed to have done here all that could be desired. The histories of Socrates, Sozomen, and Theodoret run nearly parallel, but without mutual acquaintance or dependence, and their contents are very similar.(19) Evagrius carried the narrative down to the close of the sixth century. All of them combine ecclesiastical and political history, which after Constantine were inseparably interwoven in the East; and (with the exception of Philostorgius) all occupy essentially the same orthodox stand-point. They ignore the Western church, except where it comes in contact with the East.

These successors of Eusebius are:

Socrates, an attorney or scholasticus in Constantinople, born in 380. His work, in seven books, covers the period from 306 to 439, and is valuable for its numerous extracts from sources, and its calm, impartial representation. It has been charged with a leaning towards Novatianism. He had upon the whole a higher view of the duty of the historian than his contemporaries and successors; he judged more liberally of heretics and schismatics, and is less extravagant in the praise of emperors and bishops.(20)

Hermias Sozomen, a native of Palestine, a junior contemporary of Socrates, and likewise a scholasticus in Constantinople, wrote the history of the church, in nine books, from 323 to the death of Honorius in 423,(21) and hence in its subjects keeps pace for the most part with Socrates, though, as it would appear, without the knowledge of his work, and with many additions on the history of the hermits and monks, for whom he had a great predilection.(22)

Theodoret, bishop of Cyrus, was born at Antioch about 390, of an honorable and pious mother; educated in the cloister of St. Euprepius (perhaps with Nestorius); formed upon the writings of Diodorus of Tarsus and Theodore of Mopsuestia; made bishop of Cyros, or Cyrrhos, in Syria, after 420; and died in 457. He is known to us from the Christological controversies as the most scholarly advocate of the Antiochian dyophysitism or moderate Nestorianism; condemned at Ephesus in 431, deposed by the council of Robbers in 449, acquitted in 451 by the fourth ecumenical council on condition of his condemning Nestorius and all deniers of the theotokos, but again partially condemned at the fifth long after his death. He was, therefore, like Eusebius, an actor as well as an author of church history. As bishop, he led an exemplary life, his enemies themselves being judges, and was especially benevolent to the poor. He owned nothing valuable but books, and applied the revenues of his bishopric to the public good. He shared the superstitions and weaknesses of his age.

His Ecclesiastical History, in five books, composed about 450, reaches from 325 to 429. It is the most valuable continuation of Eusebius, and, though shorter, it furnishes an essential supplement to the works of Socrates and Sozomen.

His "Historia religiosa" consists of biographies of hermits and monks, written with great enthusiasm for ascetic holiness, and with many fabulous accessories, according to the taste of the day. His "Heretical Fables,"(23) though superficial and marred by many errors, is of some importance for the history of Christian doctrine. It contains a severe condemnation of Nestorius, which we should hardly expect from Theodoret.(24)

Theodoret was a very fruitful author. Besides these histories, he wrote valuable commentaries on most of the books of the Old Testament and on all the Epistles of Paul; dogmatic and polemic works against Cyril and the Alexandrian Christology, and against the heretics; an apology of Christianity against the Greek philosophy; and sermons and letters.(25)

Evagrius (born about 536 in Syria, died after 594) was a lawyer in Antioch, and rendered the patriarch Gregory great service, particularly in an action for incest in 588. He was twice married, and the Antiochians celebrated his second wedding (592) with public plays. He is the last continuator of Eusebius and Theodoret, properly so called. He begins his Ecclesiastical History of six books with the council of Ephesus, 431, and closes it with the twelfth year of the reign of the emperor Maurice, 594. He is of special importance on the Nestorian and Eutychian controversies; gives accounts of bishops and monks, churches and public buildings, earthquakes and other calamities; and interweaves political history, such as the wars of Chosroes and the assaults of the barbarians.(26) He was strictly orthodox, and a superstitious venerator of monks, saints, and relics.(27)

Theodorus Lector, reader in the church of Constantinople about 525, compiled an abstract from Socrates, Sozomen, and Theodoret, under the title of Historia tripartita, which is still extant in the manuscript;(28) and composed a continuation of Socrates from 431 to 518, of which fragments only are preserved in John Damascenus, Nilus, and Nicephorus Callisti.(29)

Of Philostorgius, an Arian church historian (born in 368), nothing has come down to us but fragments in Photius; and these breathe so strong a partisan spirit, that the loss of the rest is not to be regretted. He described the period from the commencement of the Arian controversy to the reign of Valentinian III. a.d. 423.

The series of the Greek church historians closes with Nicephorus Callistus or Callisti (i.e., son of Callistus),(30) who lived at Constantinople in the fifteenth century. He was surprised that the voice of history had been silent since the sixth century, and resumed the long-neglected task where his predecessors had left it, but on a more extended plan of a general history of the catholic church from the beginning to the year 911. We have, however, only eighteen books to the death of emperor Phocas in 610, and a list of contents of five other books. He made large use of Eusebius and his successors, and added unreliable traditions of the later days of the Apostles, the history of Monophysitism, of monks and saints, of the barbarian irruptions, &c. He, too, ignores the Pelagian controversy, and takes little notice of the Latin church after the fifth century.(31)

In the Latin church—to anticipate thus much—Eusebius found only one imitator and continuator, the presbyter and monk Rufinus, of Aquileia (330–410). He was at first a friend of Jerome, afterwards a bitter enemy. He translated, with abridgments and insertions at his pleasure, the Ecclesiastical History of Eusebius, and continued it to Theodosius the Great (392). Yet his continuation has little value. He wrote also biographies of hermits; an exposition of the Apostles’ Creed; and translations of several works of Origen, with emendations of offensive portions.(32)

Cassiodorus, consul and monk (died about 562), composed a useful abstract of the works of Socrates, Sozomen, and Theodoret, in twelve books, under the title of Historia tripartita, for the Latin church of the middle age.

The only properly original contributions to church history from among the Latin divines were those of Jerome († 419) in his biographical and literary Catalogue of Illustrious Men (written in 392), which Gennadius, a Semi-Pelagian presbyter of South Gaul, continued to the year 495. Sulpicius Severus († 420) wrote in good style a Sacred History, or History of the Old and New Testament, from the creation down to the year 400; and Paulus Orosius (about 415) an apologetic Universal History, which hardly, however, deserves the name of a history.

§ 163. Athanasius the Great.

I. S. Athanasius: Opera omnia quae extant vel quae ejus nomine circumferuntur, etc., Gr. et lat., opera et studio monachorum ordinis S. Benedicti e congregatione S. Mauri (Jac. Lopin et B. de, Montfaucon). Paris, 1698. 3 tom. fol. (or rather 2 tomi, the first in two parts). This is the most elegant and correct edition, but must be completed by two volumes of the Collectio nova Patrum, ed. B. de Montfaucon. Par. 1706. 2 tom. fol. More complete, but not so handsome, is the edition of 1777, Patav., in 4 vols. fol. (Brunet says of the latter "Édition moins belle et moins chère quo cello de Paris, mais augmentée d’un 4e vol., lequel renferme les opuscules de S. Athan., tirés de la Collectio nova du P. Montfaucon et des Anecdota de Wolf, et de plus l’interpretatio Psalmorum.") But now both these older editions need again to be completed by the Syrian Festal Letters of Athanasius, discovered by Dr. Tattam in a Nitrian monastery in 1843; edited by W. Cureton in Syriac and English at London in 1846 and 1848 (and in English by H. Burgess and H. Williams, Oxf. 1854, in the Libr. of the Fathers); in German, with notes by F. Larson, at Leipzig in 1852; and in Syriac and Latin by Card. Angelo Mai in the Nova Patr. Bibliotheca, Rom. 1853, tom. vi. pp. 1–168. A new and more salable, though less accurate, edition of the Opera omnia Athan. (a reprint of the Benedictine) appeared at Petit-Montrouge (Par.) in J.P. Migne’s Patrologia Gr. (tom. xxv.-xxviii.), 1857, in 4 vols.

The more important dogmatic works of Athanasius have been edited separately by J. C. Thilo, in the first volume of the Bibliotheca Patrum Graec. dogmatica, Lips. 1853; and in an English translation, with explanations and indexes, by J. H. Newman, Oxf. 1842–’44 (Library of the Fathers, vols. 8, 13, 19).

  1. Gregorius Naz.: Oratio panegyrica in Magnum Athanasium (Orat. xxi.). Several Vitae Athan. in the 1st vol. of the Bened. ed. of his Opera. Acta Sanctorum for May 2d. G. Hermant: La Vie d’Athanase, etc. Par. 1679. 2 vols. Tillemont: Mémoires, vol. viii. pp. 2–258 (2d ed. Par. 1713). W. Cave: Lives of the most eminent Fathers of the first Four Centuries, vol. ii. pp. 145–364 (Oxf. ed. of 1840). Schröckh: Th. xii. pp. 101–270. J. A. Möhler: Athanasius der Grosse und die Kirche seiner Zeit, besonders im Kampfe mit dem Arianismus. Mainz, 1827. 2d (title) ed. 1844. Heinrich Voigt: Die Lehre des heiligen Athanasius von Alexandria oder die kirchliche Dogmatik des 4ten Jahrhunderts auf Grund der biblischen Lehre vom Logos. Bremen, 1861. A. P. Stanley: Lectures on the History of the Eastern Church. New York, 1862, lecture vii. (pp. 322–358).

Athanasius is the theological and ecclesiastical centre, as his senior contemporary Constantine is the political and secular, about which the Nicene age revolves. Both bear the title of the Great; the former with the better right, that his greatness was intellectual and moral, and proved itself in suffering, and through years of warfare against mighty, errors and against the imperial court. Athanasius contra mundum, et mundus contra Athanasium, is a well-known sentiment which strikingly expresses his fearless independence and immovable fidelity to his convictions. He seems to stand an unanswerable contradiction to the catholic maxim of authority: Quod sem per, quod ubique, quod ab omnibus creditum est, and proves that truth is by no means always on the side of the majority, but may often be very unpopular. The solitary Athanasius even in exile, and under the ban of council and emperor, was the bearer of the truth, and, as he was afterwards named, the "father of orthodoxy."(33)

On a martyrs’ day in 313 the bishop Alexander of Alexandria saw a troop of boys imitating the church services in innocent sport, Athanasius playing the part of bishop, and performing baptism by immersion.(34) He caught in this a glimpse of future greatness; took the youth into his care; and appointed him his secretary, and afterwards his archdeacon. Athanasius studied the classics, the Holy Scriptures, and the church fathers, and meantime lived as an ascetic. He already sometimes visited St. Anthony in his solitude.

In the year 325 he accompanied his bishop to the council of Nicaea, and at once distinguished himself there by his zeal and ability in refuting Arianism and vindicating the eternal deity of Christ, and incurred the hatred of this heretical party, which raised so many storms about his life.

In the year 328(35) he was nominated to the episcopal succession of Alexandria, on the recommendation of the dying Alexander, and by the voice of the people, though not yet of canonical age, and at first disposed to avoid the election by flight; and thus he was raised to the highest ecclesiastical dignity of the East. For the bishop of Alexandria was at the same time metropolitan of Egypt, Libya, and Pentapolis.

But now immediately began the long series of contests with the Arian party, which had obtained influence at the court of Constantine, and had induced the emperor to recall Arius and his adherents from exile. Henceforth the personal fortunes of Athanasius are so inseparably interwoven with the history of the Arian controversy that Nicene and Athanasian are equivalent terms, and the different depositions and restorations of Athanasius denote so many depressions and victories of the Nicene orthodoxy. Five times did the craft and power of his opponents, upon the pretext of all sorts of personal and political offences, but in reality on account of his inexorable opposition to the Arian and Semi-Arian heresy, succeed in deposing and banishing him. The first exile he spent in Treves, the second chiefly in Rome, the third with the monks in the Egyptian desert; and he employed them in the written defence of his righteous cause. Then the Arian party, was distracted, first by internal division, and further by the death of the emperor Constantius (361), who was their chief support. The pagan Julian recalled the banished bishops of both parties, in the hope that they might destroy one another. Thus, Athanasius among them, who was the most downright opposite of the Christian-hating emperor, again received his bishopric. But when, by his energetic and wise administration, he rather restored harmony in his diocese, and sorely injured paganism, which he feared far less than Arianism, and thus frustrated the cunning plan of Julian, the emperor resorted to violence, and banished him as a dangerous disturber of the peace. For the fourth time Athanasius left Alexandria, but calmed his weeping friends with the prophetic words: "Be of good cheer; it is only a cloud, which will soon pass over." By presence of mind he escaped from an imperial ship on the Nile, which had two hired assassins on board. After Julian’s death in 362 he was again recalled by Jovian. But the next emperor Valens, an Arian, issued in 367 an edict which again banished all the bishops who had been deposed under Constantius and restored by Julian. The aged Athanasius was obliged for the fifth time to leave his beloved flock, and kept himself concealed more than four months in the tomb of his father. Then Valens, boding ill from the enthusiastic adherence of the Alexandrians to their orthodox bishop, repealed the edict.

From this time Athanasius had peace, and still wrote, at a great age, with the vigor of youth, against Apollinarianism. In the year 373(36) he died, after an administration of nearly forty-six years, but before the conclusion of the Arian war. He had secured by his testimony the final victory of orthodoxy, but, like Moses, was called away from the earthly scene before the goal was reached.

Athanasius, like many great men (from David and Paul to Napoleon and Schleiermacher), was very small of stature,(37) somewhat stooping and emaciated by fasting and many troubles, but fair of countenance, with a piercing eye and a personal appearance of great power even over his enemies.(38) His omnipresent activity, his rapid and his mysterious movements, his fearlessness, and his prophetic insight into the future, were attributed by his friends to divine assistance, by his enemies to a league with evil powers. Hence the belief in his magic art.(39) His congregation in Alexandria and the people and monks of Egypt were attached to him through all the vicissitudes of his tempestuous life with equal fidelity and veneration. Gregory Nazianzen begins his enthusiastic panegyric with the words: "When I praise Athanasius, I praise virtue itself, because he combines all virtues in himself." Constantine the Younger called him "the man of God;" Theodoret, "the great enlightener;" and John of Damascus, the corner-stone of the church of God."

All this is, indeed, very hyperbolical, after the fashion of degenerate Grecian rhetoric. Athanasius was not free from the faults of his age. But he is, on the whole, one of the purest, most imposing, and most venerable personages in the history of the church; and this judgment will now be almost universally accepted.(40)

He was (and there are few such) a theological and churchly character in magnificent, antique style. He was a man of one mould and one idea, and in this respect one-sided; yet in the best sense, as the same is true of most great men who are borne along with a mighty and comprehensive thought, and subordinate all others to it. So Paul lived and labored for Christ crucified, Gregory VII. for the Roman hierarchy, Luther for the doctrine of justification by faith, Calvin for the idea of the sovereign grace of God. It was the passion and the life-work of Athanasius to vindicate the deity of Christ, which he rightly regarded as the corner-stone of the edifice of the Christian faith, and without which he could conceive no redemption. For this truth he spent all his time and strength; for this he suffered deposition and twenty years of exile; for this he would have been at any moment glad to pour out his blood. For his vindication of this truth he was much hated, much loved, always respected or feared. In the unwavering conviction that he had the right and the protection of God on his side, he constantly disdained to call in the secular power for his ecclesiastical ends, and to degrade himself to an imperial courtier, as his antagonists often did.

Against the Arians he was inflexible, because he believed they hazarded the essence of Christianity itself, and he allowed himself the most invidious and the most contemptuous terms. He calls them polytheists, atheists, Jews, Pharisees, Sadducees, Herodians, spies, worse persecutors than the heathen, liars, dogs, wolves, antichrists, and devils. But he confined himself to spiritual weapons, and never, like his successor Cyril a century later, used nor counselled measures of force. He suffered persecution, but did not practise it; he followed the maxim: Orthodoxy should persuade faith, not force it.

Towards the unessential errors of good men, like those of Marcellus of Ancyra, he was indulgent. Of Origen he spoke with esteem, and with gratitude for his services, while Epiphanius, and even Jerome, delighted to blacken his memory and burn his bones. To the suspicions of the orthodoxy of Basil, whom, by the way, be never personally knew, he gave no ear, but pronounced his liberality a justifiable condescension to the weak. When he found himself compelled to write against Apollinaris, whom he esteemed and loved, he confined himself to the refutation of his error, without the mention of his name. He was more concerned for theological ideas than for words and formulas; even upon the shibboleth homoousios he would not obstinately insist, provided only the great truth of the essential and eternal Godhead of Christ were not sacrificed. At his last appearance in public, as president of the council of Alexandria in 362, he acted as mediator and reconciler of the contending parties, who, notwithstanding all their discord in the use of the terms ousia and hypostasis, were one in the ground-work of their faith.

No one of all the Oriental fathers enjoyed so high consideration in the Western church as Athanasius. His personal sojourn in Rome and Treves, and his knowledge of the Latin tongue, contributed to this effect. He transplanted monasticism to the West. But it was his advocacy of the fundamental doctrine of Christianity that, more than all, gave him his Western reputation. Under his name the Symbolum Quicunque, of much later, and probably of French, origin, has found universal acceptance in the Latin church, and has maintained itself to this day in living use. His name is inseparable from the conflicts and the triumph of the doctrine of the holy Trinity.

As an author, Athanasius is distinguished for theological depth and discrimination, for dialectical skill, and sometimes for fulminating eloquence. He everywhere evinces a triumphant intellectual superiority over his antagonists, and shows himself a veritable malleus haereticorum. He pursues them into all their hiding-places, and refutes all their arguments and their sophisms, but never loses sight of the main point of the controversy, to which he ever returns with renewed force. His views are governed by a strict logical connection; but his stormy fortunes prevented him from composing a large systematic work. Almost all his writings are occasional, wrung from him by circumstances; not a few of them were hastily written in exile.

They may be divided as follows:

  1. Apologetic works in defence of Christianity. Among these are the two able and enthusiastic kindred productions of his youth (composed before 325): "A Discourse against the Greeks," and "On the Incarnation of the Divine Word,)"(41) which he already looked upon as the central idea of the Christian religion.

  2. Dogmatic and Controversial works in defence of the Nicene faith; which are at the same time very important to the history of the Arian controversies. Of these the following are directed against Arianism: An Encyclical Letter to all Bishops (written in 341); On the Decrees of the Council of Nicaea (352); On the Opinion of Dionysius of Alexandria (352); An Epistle to the Bishops of Egypt and Libya (356); four Orations against the Arians (358); A Letter to Serapion on the Death of Arius (358 or 359); A History of the Arians to the Monks (between 358 and 360). To these are to be added four Epistles to Serapion on the Deity of the Holy Spirit (358), and two books Against Apollinaris, in defence of the full humanity of Christ (379).

  3. Works in his own Personal Defence: An Apology against the Arians (350); an Apology to Constantius (356); an Apology concerning his Flight (De fuga, 357 or 358); and several letters.

  4. Exegetical works; especially a Commentary on the Psalms, in which he everywhere finds types and prophecies of Christ and the church, according to the extravagant allegorizing method of the Alexandrian school; and a synopsis or compendium of the Bible. But the genuineness of these unimportant works is by many doubted.(42)

  5. Ascetic and Practical works. Chief among these are his "Life of St. Anthony," composed about 365, or at all events after the death of Anthony,(43) and his "Festal Letters," which have but recently become known.(44) The Festal Letters give us a glimpse of his pastoral fidelity as bishop, and throw new light also on many of his doctrines, and on the condition of the church in his time. In these letters Athanasius, according to Alexandrian custom, announced annually, at Epiphany, to the clergy and congregations of Egypt, the time of the next Easter, and added edifying observations on passages of Scripture, and timely exhortations. These were read in the churches, during the Easter season, especially on Palm-Sunday. As Athanasius was bishop forty-five years, he would have written that number of Festal Letters, if he had not been several times prevented by flight or sickness. The letters were written in Greek, but soon translated into Syriac, and lay buried for centuries in the dust of a Nitrian cloister, till the research of Protestant Scholarship brought them again to the light.

§ 164. Basil the Great.

I. S. Basilius Caes. Cappad. archiepisc.: Opera omnia quae exstant vel quae ejus nomine circumferuntur, Gr. et Lat. ed. Jul. Garnier, presbyter and monk of the Bened. order. Paris, 1721–’30. 3 vols. fol. Eadem ed. Parisina altera, emendata et aucta a Lud. de Sinner, Par. (Gaume Fratres) 1839, 8 tomi in 6 Partes (an elegant and convenient ed.). Reprinted also by Migne, Par. 1857, in 4 vols. (Patrol. Gr. tom xxix, xxxii.). The first edition of St. Basil was superintended by Erasmus with Froben in Basle, 1532. Comp. also Opera Bas. dogmatica selecta in Thilo’s Bibl. Patr. Gr. dogm. vol. ii. Lips. 1854 (under care of J. D. H. Goldhorn, and containing the Libri iii. adversus Eunomium, and Liber i. de Spiritu Sancto).

  1. Ancient accounts and descriptions of Basil in the funeral discourses and eulogies of Gregory Naz. (Oratio xliii.), Gregory Nyss., Amphilochius, Ephraem Syrus. Garnier: Vita S. Basilii, in his edition of the Opera, tom. iii. pp. xxxviii.-ccliv. (in the new Paris ed. of 1839; or tom. i. in Migne’s reprint). Comp. also the Vitae in the Acta Sanctorum, sub Jan. 14, by Hermant, Tillemont (tom. ix.), Fabricius (Bibl. tom. ix.), Cave, Pfeiffer, Schröckh (Part xiii. pp. 8–220), Böhringer, W. Klose (Basilius der Grosse, Stralsund, 1835), and Fialon (Etude historique et littéraire sur S. Basile, Par. 1866).

The Asiatic province of Cappadocia produced in the fourth century the three distinguished church teachers, Basil and the two Gregories, who stand in strong contrast with the general character of their countrymen; for the Cappadocians are described as a cowardly, servile, and deceitful race.(45)

Basil was born about the year 329,(46) at Caesarea, the capital of Cappadocia, in the bosom of a wealthy and pious family, whose ancestors had distinguished themselves as martyrs. The seed of piety had been planted in him by his grandmother, St. Macrina, and his mother, St. Emmelia. He had four brothers and five sisters, who all led a religious life; two of his brothers, Gregory, bishop of Nyssa, and Peter, bishop of Sebaste, and his sister, Macrina the Younger, are, like himself, among the saints of the Eastern church. He received his literary education at first from his father, who was a rhetorician; afterwards at school in Constantinople (347), where he enjoyed the instruction and personal esteem of the celebrated Libanius; and in Athens, where he spent several years, between 351 and 355,(47) studying rhetoric, mathematics, and philosophy, in company with his intimate friend Gregory Nazianzen, and at the same time with prince Julian the Apostate.

Athens, partly through its ancient renown and its historical traditions, partly by excellent teachers of philosophy and eloquence, Sophists, as they were called in an honorable sense, among whom Himerius and Proaeresius were at that time specially conspicuous, was still drawing a multitude of students from all quarters of Greece, and even from the remote provinces of Asia. Every Sophist had his own school and party, which was attached to him with incredible zeal, and endeavored to gain every newly arriving student to its master. In these efforts, as well as in the frequent literary contests and debates of the various schools among themselves, there was not seldom much rude and wild behavior. To youth who were not yet firmly grounded in Christianity, residence in Athens, and occupation with the ancient classics, were full of temptation, and might easily kindle an enthusiasm for heathenism, which, however, had already lost its vitality, and was upheld solely by the artificial means of magic, theurgy, and an obscure mysticism.(48)

Basil and Gregory remained steadfast, and no poetical or rhetorical glitter could fade the impressions of a pious training. Gregory says of their studies in Athens, in his forty-third Oration:(49) "We knew only two streets of the city, the first and the more excellent one to the churches, and to the ministers of the altar; the other, which, however, we did not so highly esteem, to the public schools and to the teachers of the sciences. The streets to the theatres, games, and places of unholy amusements, we left to others. Our holiness was our great concern; our sole aim was to be called and to be Christians. In this we placed our whole glory."(50) In a later oration on classic studies Basil encourages them, but admonishes that they should be pursued with caution, and with constant regard to the great Christian purpose of eternal life, to which all earthly objects and attainments are as shadows and dreams to reality. In plucking the rose one should beware of the thorns, and, like the bee, should not only delight himself with the color and the fragrance, but also gain useful honey from the flower.(51)

The intimate friendship of Basil and Gregory, lasting from fresh, enthusiastic youth till death, resting on an identity of spiritual and moral aims, and sanctified by Christian piety, is a lovely and engaging chapter in the history of the fathers, and justifies a brief episode in a field not yet entered by any church historian.

With all the ascetic narrowness of the time, which fettered even these enlightened fathers, they still had minds susceptible to science and art and the beauties of nature. In the works of Basil and of the two Gregories occur pictures of nature such as we seek in vain in the heathen classics. The descriptions of natural scenery among the poets and philosophers of ancient Greece and Rome can be easily compressed within a few pages. Socrates, as we learn from Plato, was of the opinion that we can learn nothing from trees and fields, and hence he never took a walk; he was so bent upon self-knowledge, as the true aim of all learning, that he regarded the whole study of nature as useless, because it did not tend to make man either more intelligent or more virtuous. The deeper sense of the beauty of nature is awakened by the religion of revelation alone, which teaches us to see everywhere in creation the traces of the power, the wisdom, and the goodness of God. The book of Ruth, the book of Job, many Psalms, particularly the 104th, and the parables, are without parallel in Grecian or Roman literature. The renowned naturalist, Alexander von Humboldt, collected some of the most beautiful descriptions of nature from the fathers for his purposes.(52) They are an interesting proof of the transfiguring power of the spirit of Christianity even upon our views of nature.

A breath of sweet sadness runs through them, which is entirely foreign to classical antiquity. This is especially manifest in Gregory of Nyssa, the brother of Basil. "When I see," says he, for example, "every rocky ridge, every valley, every plain, covered with new-grown grass; and then the variegated beauty of the trees, and at my feet the lilies doubly enriched by nature with sweet odors and gorgeous colors; when I view in the distance the sea, to which the changing cloud leads out—my soul is seized with sadness which is not without delight. And when in autumn fruits disappear, leaves fall, boughs stiffen, stripped of their beauteous dress—we sink with the perpetual and regular vicissitude into the harmony of wonder-working nature. He who looks through this with the thoughtful eye of the soul, feels the littleness of man in the greatness of the universe."(53) Yet we find sunny pictures also, like the beautiful description of spring in an oration of Gregory Nazianzen on the martyr Mamas.(54)

A second characteristic of these representations of nature, and for the church historian the most important, is the reference of earthly beauty to an eternal and heavenly principle, and that glorification of God in the works of creation, which transplanted itself from the Psalms and the book of Job into the Christian church. In his homilies on the history of the Creation, Basil describes the mildness of the serene nights in Asia Minor, where the stars, "the eternal flowers of heaven, raised the spirit of man from the visible to the invisible." In the oration just mentioned, after describing the spring in the most lovely and life-like colors, Gregory Nazianzen proceeds: "Everything praises God and glorifies Him with unutterable tones; for everything shall thanks be offered also to God by me, and thus shall the song of those creatures, whose song of praise I here utter, be also ours .... Indeed it is now [alluding to the Easter festival] the spring-time of the world, the spring-time of the spirit, spring-time for souls, spring-time for bodies, a visible spring, an invisible spring, in which we also shall there have part, if we here be rightly transformed, and enter as new men upon a new life." Thus the earth becomes a vestibule of heaven, the beauty of the body is consecrated an image of the beauty of the spirit.

The Greek fathers placed the beauty of nature above the works of art, having a certain prejudice against art on account of the heathen abuses of it. "If thou seest a splendid building, and the view of its colonnades would transport thee, look quickly at the vault of the heavens and the open fields, on which the flocks are feeding on the shore of the sea. Who does not despise every creation of art, when in the silence of the heart he early wonders at the rising sun, as it pours its golden (crocus-yellow) light over the horizon? when, resting at a spring in the deep grass or under the dark shade of thick trees, he feeds his eye upon the dim vanishing distance?"
